Long-time asymptotic behavior of the Novikov equation in space-time solitonic regions
Yiling YANG, Engui FAN

Abstract
In this paper, we study the long time asymptotic behavior for the Cauchy problem of the Novikov equation with 3 × 3 3\times 3 matrix spectral problem u t − u t ​ x ​ x + 4 ​ u x = 3 ​ u ​ u x ​ u x ​ x + u 2 ​ u x ​ x ​ x , \displaystyle u_{t}-u_{txx}+4u_{x}=3uu_{x}u_{xx}+u^{2}u_{xxx}, u ​ ( x , 0 ) = u 0 ​ ( x ) , \displaystyle u(x,0)=u_{0}(x), where u 0 ​ ( x ) u_{0}(x) u 0 ​ ( x ) → κ > 0 , x → ± ∞ u_{0}(x)\rightarrow\kappa>0,\ x\rightarrow\pm\infty and u 0 ​ ( x ) − κ u_{0}(x)-\kappa is assumed in the Schwarz space. It is shown that the solution of the Cauchy problem can be characterized via a Riemann-Hilbert problem in a new scale ( y , t ) (y,t) with y = x − ∫ x ∞ ( ( u − u x ​ x + 1 ) 2 / 3 − 1 ) ​ 𝑑 s . y=x-\int_{x}^{\infty}\left((u-u_{xx}+1)^{2/3}-1\right)ds. In different space-time solitonic regions of ξ = y / t ∈ ( − ∞ , − 1 / 8 ) ∪ ( 1 , + ∞ ) \xi=y/t\in(-\infty,-1/8)\cup(1,+\infty) and ξ ∈ ( − 1 / 8 , 1 ) \xi\in(-1/8,1) , we apply ∂ ¯ \overline{\partial} steepest descent method to obtain the different long time asymptotic expansions of the solution u ⁡ ( y , t ) u(y,t) . The corresponding residual error order is 𝒪 ⁡ ( t − 1 + ρ ) \mathcal{O}(t^{-1+\rho}) and 𝒪 ( t − 3 / 4 ) \mathcal{O}(t^{-3/4}) respectively from a ∂ ¯ \overline{\partial} -equation. Our result implies that soliton resolution can be characterized with an N ⁡ ( Λ ) N(\Lambda) -soliton whose parameters are modulated by a sum of localized soliton-soliton interactions as one moves through the regions. Keywords: Novikov equation; Riemann-Hilbert problem, ∂ ¯ \overline{\partial} steepest descent method. MSC: 35Q51; 35Q15; 37K15; 35C20.

1
Introduction

In this paper, we study the long time asymptotic behavior for the initial value problem on a nonzero background for the
Novikov equation

\displaystyle u_{t}-u_{txx}+4u_{x}=3uu_{x}u_{xx}+u^{2}u_{xxx},

(1.1)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=u_{0}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\ t>0,

(1.2)

\displaystyle u_{0}(x)\to\kappa>0,\ x\to\pm\infty,

(1.3)

where
u=u(x,t)
is a real-valued function of
x
and
t
. By introducing the momentum variable
m=u-u_{xx}
, the Novikov equation (
1.1
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle m_{t}+\left(m_{x}u+3mu_{x}\right)u=0,

(1.4)

or equivalently,

\displaystyle(m^{2/3})_{t}+\left(u^{2}m^{2/3}\right)_{x}=0.

(1.5)

The Novikov equation (
1.1
) as a new integrable system was derived in the search for a classification of integrable
generalized Camassa-Holm equations of the form

\displaystyle(1-\partial_{x}^{2})u_{t}=F(u,u_{x},u_{xx},...)

(1.6)

possessing infinite hierarchies of higher symmetries. A scalar Lax pair involving the third order derivative with respect to
x

was given
[
1
,
2
]
. Further, by using the prolongation algebra method,
Hone and Wang proposed a
3\times 3
matrix Lax pair and a bi-Hamiltonian structure for the Novikov equation (
1.1
)
[
3
]
. This Lax pair was used
to construct explicit peakon solutions on zero background, which replicates a feature characterizing
the waves of great height-waves of largest amplitude that were exact solutions of the governing equations for water waves
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
.
Hone et al. further derived the
explicit formulas for multipeakon solutions of the Novikov equation (
1.1
)
[
8
]
. By using the Hirota bilinear method,
Matsuno presented parametric representations of smooth multisoliton solutions
for the Novikov equation (
1.1
) on a nonzero constant background
[
9
]
.
He also demonstrated that a smooth soliton converged to a peakon in the limit where the constant background tended to
0
while the velocity of the soliton is fixed.
Wu et al. obtained N-soliton solutions to the Novikov equation through Darboux transformations
[
10
]
. Recently, Chang et al.
applied Pfaffian technique to study multipeakons of the Novikov equation,
a link between the Novikov peakons and the finite Toda lattice of BKP type as well as Hermite-Pade approximation to the Novikov peakon problem

[
11
,
12
]
. Boutet de Monvel et al. developed the inverse scattering theory to
the Novikov equation (
1.1
) with nonzero constant background
[
13
]
, where under a simple transformation

\displaystyle u(x,t)\rightarrow\kappa\tilde{u}(x-\kappa^{2}t,\kappa^{2}t)+\kappa,

(1.7)

the Cauchy problem of Novikov equation (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) reduces to the following new Cauchy
problem on zero background

\displaystyle(\tilde{m}^{2/3})_{t}+\left(\tilde{m}^{2/3}\left(u^{2}+2u\right)\right)_{x}=0,\ \tilde{m}=u-u_{xx}+1,

(1.8)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=u_{0}(x).

(1.9)

where
u_{0}(x)
satisfies the sign condition

u_{0}(x)-u_{0,xx}(x)+1>0.

Then there exists a unique global solution
u(x,t)
of the Novikov equation (
1.1
),
such that
u(x,t)\to 0
as
x\to\pm\infty
for all
t>0

[
14
]
.

The Novikov equation (
1.8
) also admits
3\times 3
matrix spectral problem as the Sasa-Satuma equation, Degasperis-Procesi (DP) equation, good Boussinesq equation, three-wave equation

[
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
13
,
44
,
15
,
45
,
46
,
47
]
.
However, the Novikov equation and DP equation possess numerous common characteristics in their
Riemann-Hilbert (RH) problem and face some difficulties
[
13
]
.
One of the difficulties is the Lax pair associated with (
1.8
) has six singularities at
\varkappa_{n}=e^{\frac{n\pi i}{3}}
for
n=1,...,6
.
Boutet de Monvel et al. developed a steepest descent approach to obtain the long time asymptotic behavior for the DP
equation
[
15
]
. However, to the best of our knowledge, the
the long time asymptotics of the Novikov equation is still not presented yet.

0
y
t
0<\xi<1
-1/8<\xi<0
\xi>1
\xi<-1/8
\xi=-1/8
\xi=1

Figure 1:
Asymptotic approximations of the Novikov equation in different space-time solitonic regions,
where without stationary phase points in yellow region; 12 and 24 stationary phase points in green and blue regions respectively.

Motivated by their work
[
13
,
15
]
, we carry out the long-time analysis
for the Novikov equation in this paper. We obtain different leading order asymptotic approximation for the Novikov equation (
1.8
)
in different space-time solitonic regions
(see Figure
1
and the detail results are given in the Theorem
1
in the section 8). Our main tool is the nonlinear
\bar{\partial}
steepest approach introduced by McLaughlin-Miller,
which was first applied to analyze asymptotic of orthogonal polynomials
[
28
,
29
]
,
later applied to analyze long time asymptotics of integrable systems
[
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
.
The first work on the long-time behavior of nonlinear wave equations with inverse scattering method
was carried out by Manakov
[
16
]
.
Later, this method was applied to other integrable systems
such as KdV, Landau-Lifshitz and the reduced Maxwell-Bloch system
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
.
In 1993,
Deift and Zhou developed a nonlinear steepest descent method to rigorously obtain the long-time asymptotics behavior of the solution for the mKdV equation

[
21
]
. Since then this method
has been widely applied on the NLS equation, KdV equation, derivative NLS equation,
Fokas-Lenells equation, short-pulse equation and Camassa-Holm equation, etc.
[
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
.

Our paper is arranged as follows. In section
2
, we recall some main results on
the construction process of RH problem
[
13
]
, which will be used
to analyze long-time asymptotics of the Novikov equation in our paper. Further we demonstrate that the norm of reflection
coefficient

\parallel r(z)\parallel_{L^{\infty}}<1
to ensure the existence of solutions of the RH problem
M(z)
.
In section
3
,
a
T(z)
function is introduced to normalize the RH problem

M(z)
into a new RH problem for
M^{(1)}(z)
, which admits a regular discrete spectrum and two triangular decompositions of the jump matrix
near
z=0
.
In section
4
, by introducing a matrix-valued function
R(z)
to make a continuous extension of
M^{(1)}(z)

into a hybrid
\bar{\partial}
-RH problem for
M^{(2)}(z)
.
Further we decompose
M^{(2)}(z)
into a
pure RH problem for
M^{R}(z)
and a pure
\bar{\partial}
Problem for
M^{(3)}(z)
.
We find that the contribution to the
M^{R}(z)
come from three aspects (
two aspects in the case without stationary phase points).
The first contribution comes from
discrete spectrum, where a modified reflectionless RH problem
M^{(r)}(z)
for the soliton components is solved in Section
5
.
The second contribution in Section
6
comes from jump contours, which is approximated by a local solvable model
M^{lo}(z)
.
The third contribution is from a pure jump RH problem near singularities
\varkappa_{n}
.
Finally the residual error function is is given with a small RH problem.
In Section
7
, we analyze the
\bar{\partial}
-problem for
M^{(3)}(z)
.
Finally, in Section
8
, based on the result obtained above, a relation formula
is found

\displaystyle M(z)=M^{(3)}(z)E(z)M^{(r)}(z)R^{(2)}(z)^{-1}T(z)^{-1},

from which we then obtain the long-time asymptotic behavior for the Novikov equation (
1.8
) via a reconstruction formula.

2
Direct scattering and basic RH problem

To state our results precisely we introduce notation and function spaces used in this paper.
If
I
is an interval on the real line
\mathbb{R}
and
X
is a Banach space, then
C^{0}(I,X)
denotes the space of continuous functions on
I
taking values in
X
. It is equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{C^{0}(I,X)}=\sup_{x\in I}\|f(x)\|_{X}.

Besides, we denote
C^{0}_{B}(X)
as a space of bounded continuous functions on
X
.

If the entries
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are in space
X
, then we call vector
\vec{f}=(f_{1},f_{2})^{T}
is in space
X
with
\parallel\vec{f}\parallel_{X}\triangleq\parallel f_{1}\parallel_{X}+\parallel f_{2}\parallel_{X}
. Similarly, if every entries of matrix
A
are in space
X
, then we call
A
is also in space
X
.
We introduce the normed spaces.
A weighted
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
space is specified by

L^{p,s}(\mathbb{R})=\left\{f(x)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})|\hskip 2.84544pt|x|^{s}f(x)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\right\};

A Sobolev space is defined by

W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R})=\left\{f(x)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})|\hskip 2.84544pt\partial^{j}f(x)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\text{ for }j=1,2,...,k\right\};

A weighted Sobolev space is defined by

H^{k,s}(\mathbb{R})=\left\{f(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})|\hskip 2.84544pt(1+|x|^{s})\partial^{j}f(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\text{ for }j=1,...,k\right\}.

And the norm of
f(x)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
and
g(x)\in L^{p,s}(\mathbb{R})
are abbreviated to
\parallel f\parallel_{p}
,

\parallel g\parallel_{p,s}
, respectively.

2.1
Spectral analysis

The Novikov equation (
1.8
) admits a Lax pair
[
3
,
13
]

\breve{\Phi}_{x}(k)=\breve{X}\breve{\Phi}(k),\hskip 14.22636pt\breve{\Phi}_{t}(k)=\breve{T}\breve{\Phi}(k),

(2.1)

where

\breve{X}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&k\tilde{m}&1\\
0&0&k\tilde{m}\\
1&0&0\end{array}\right),

\breve{T}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-(u+1)u_{x}+\frac{1}{3k^{2}}&\frac{u_{x}}{k}-(u^{2}+2u)k\tilde{m}&u_{x}^{2}+1\\
\frac{u+1}{k}&-\frac{2}{3k^{2}}&-\frac{u_{x}}{k}-(u^{2}+2u)k\tilde{m}\\
-u^{2}-2u&\frac{u+1}{k}&(u+1)u_{x}+\frac{1}{3k^{2}}\end{array}\right),

and
k
is a spectral parameter.
In order to control the large
k
behavior of the solutions of above Lax pair, we introduce a new transformation

\displaystyle\Phi(z)\triangleq P^{-1}(z)D^{-1}(x,t)\breve{\Phi}(k),

(2.2)

where
z
is a new uniformization variable to avoid multi-value of eigenvalue
k
and

\displaystyle D(x,t)\triangleq\text{diag}\{q(x,t),q^{-1}(x,t),1\},\quad q=q(x,t)\triangleq\tilde{m}^{1/3}(x,t),

\displaystyle P(z)\triangleq\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda_{1}^{2}(z)&\lambda_{2}^{2}(z)&\lambda_{3}^{2}(z)\\
k(z)&k(z)&k(z)\\
\lambda_{1}(z)&\lambda_{2}(z)&\lambda_{3}(z)\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle P^{-1}(z)\triangleq\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{1}{3\lambda_{1}^{2}(z)-1}&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{3\lambda_{2}^{2}(z)-1}&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{3\lambda_{3}^{2}(z)-1}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{z}{\lambda_{1}(z)}&\lambda_{1}(z)\\
1&\frac{z}{\lambda_{2}(z)}&\lambda_{2}(z)\\
1&\frac{z}{\lambda_{3}(z)}&\lambda_{3}(z)\end{array}\right).

Here
\lambda_{j}(z)
,
j=1,2,3
are three roots of the following algebraic equation

\displaystyle\lambda^{3}(z)-\lambda(z)=k^{2}(z),

(2.12)

where

\displaystyle k(z)^{2}=\frac{1}{3\sqrt{3}}\left(z^{3}+\frac{1}{z^{3}}\right),\quad\lambda_{j}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\left(\omega^{j}z+\frac{1}{\omega^{j}z}\right),\quad\omega=e^{\frac{2i\pi}{3}}.

(2.13)

Obviously,
k(\kappa_{n})=0
, for
\kappa_{n}=e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}+\frac{i\pi(n-1)}{3}}
,
n=1,...,6
.
By this transformation,
\Phi
admits a new Lax pair as

\displaystyle\Phi_{x}-q^{2}\Lambda(z)\Phi=U\Phi,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}+\left[(u^{2}+2u)q^{2}\Lambda(z)-A(z)\right]\Phi=V\Phi,

(2.15)

where

\displaystyle\Lambda(z)\triangleq\text{diag}\{\lambda_{1}(z),\lambda_{2}(z),\lambda_{3}(z)\},\ \ A(z)\triangleq\frac{1}{3k^{2}}+\Lambda(z)^{-1},

\displaystyle U\triangleq U_{1}U_{2},\ \ V\triangleq U_{1}(V_{1}+V_{2}\Lambda),

(2.16)

\displaystyle U_{1}\triangleq\text{diag}\left\{\frac{1}{3\lambda_{1}^{2}-1},\frac{1}{3\lambda_{2}^{2}-1},\frac{1}{3\lambda_{3}^{2}-1}\right\},

\displaystyle U_{2}\triangleq\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}c_{2}\lambda_{1}&c_{1}(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2})+c_{2}\lambda_{2}&c_{1}(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{3}^{2})+c_{2}\lambda_{3}\\
c_{1}(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}^{2})+c_{2}\lambda_{1}&c_{2}\lambda_{2}&c_{1}(\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}^{2})+c_{2}\lambda_{3}\\
c_{1}(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{3}^{2})+c_{2}\lambda_{1}&c_{1}(\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2})+c_{2}\lambda_{2}&c_{2}\lambda_{3}\end{array}\right),

with
c_{1}=\frac{q_{x}}{q}
and
c_{2}=q^{-2}-q^{2}
.
V_{1}
has same form of
U_{1}
with
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
replaced by
c_{3}=-(u^{2}+2u)\frac{q_{x}}{q}
and
c_{4}=(u^{2}+2u)q^{2}+\frac{u_{x}^{2}+1}{q^{2}}-1
, respectively.
And we denote
c_{5}=\frac{u_{x}}{q}
,
c_{6}=(u+1)q-1
, then
V_{2}
can be written as

\displaystyle V_{2}\triangleq[V_{2}^{(jl)}]_{3\times 3},\quad V_{2}^{(jl)}\triangleq c_{5}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{l}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}\right)+c_{6}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{l}}+\frac{\lambda_{l}}{\lambda_{j}}\right).

(2.20)

Introduce a
3\times 3
diagonal function

Q(z;x,t)=y(x,t)\Lambda(z)+tA(z),

where

\displaystyle Q_{x}=q^{2}\Lambda,\quad Q_{t}=-\left(u^{2}+2u\right)q^{2}\Lambda-A,

\displaystyle y(x,t)=x-\int_{x}^{\infty}\left(q^{2}(s,t)-1\right)ds,

(2.21)

\displaystyle c_{+}(x,t)=\int_{x}^{\infty}\left(q^{2}(s,t)-1\right)ds.

Making a transformation

\displaystyle\Phi(z;x,t)=\mu(z;x,t)e^{Q},

(2.22)

then Lax pair (
2.15
) is changed to

\displaystyle\mu_{x}-[Q_{x},\mu]=U\mu,\quad\mu_{t}-[Q_{t},\mu]=V\mu,

(2.23)

whose solutions satisfy the Fredholm integral equations

\mu^{\pm}(z;x,t)=I-\int_{x}^{\pm\infty}e^{-\hat{\Lambda}(z)\int_{x}^{s}q^{2}(v,t)dv}[U\mu_{\pm}(s,t;z)]ds.

(2.24)

We define six rays at
z=0

\Sigma=\cup_{n=1}^{6}L_{n},\ \ \ L_{n}=e^{\frac{\pi(n-1)i}{3}}\mathbb{R}^{+},\ n=1,\cdots,6,

which divide the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
into six open cones

S_{n}=\{z\in\mathbb{C};\arg z\in({(n-1)\pi}/{3},{n\pi}/{3})\},\ n=1,\cdots,6,

see Figure
2
. Denote the matrix

\mu^{\pm}=\left(\mu^{\pm}_{1},\mu^{\pm}_{2},\mu^{\pm}_{3}\right),

where
\mu^{\pm}_{1}
,
\mu^{\pm}_{3}
and
\mu^{\pm}_{3}
are
the first, second and third column of
\mu^{\pm}(z)
respectively.
Then from (
2.24
), we can show that
\left(\mu^{+}_{1},\mu^{+}_{2},\mu^{+}_{3}\right)
is analytical in the domains

\left(\bar{S}_{1}\cup\bar{S}_{2},\ \bar{S}_{5}\cup\bar{S}_{6},\ \bar{S}_{3}\cup\bar{S}_{4}\right).

And
\left(\mu^{-}_{1},\mu^{-}_{2},\mu^{-}_{3}\right)
is analytical in the domains

\left(\bar{S}_{4}\cup\bar{S}_{5},\ \bar{S}_{3}\cup\bar{S}_{2},\ \bar{S}_{1}\cup\bar{S}_{6}\right).

Here,
\bar{S}_{j}
denotes the closure of
S_{j}
for
j=1,...,6
, respectively.
The initial points of integration
\infty_{j,l}
are specified as follows for each matrix entry
(j,l)
for
j,l=1,2,3
:

\displaystyle\infty_{j,l}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}+\infty,&\text{if Re}\lambda_{j}\geq\text{Re}\lambda_{l},\\[12.0pt]
-\infty,&\text{if Re}\lambda_{j}<\text{Re}\lambda_{l}.\\
\end{array}\right.

Then (
2.24
) can be rewritten as a system of Fredholm integral equations

\mu_{jl}(z;x,t)=I_{jl}-\int_{x}^{\infty_{jl}}e^{-\int_{x}^{s}q^{2}(v,t)dv(\lambda_{j}(z)-\lambda_{l}(z))}[U\mu(s,t;z)]_{jl}ds.

(2.27)

L_{1}
L_{2}
L_{3}
L_{4}
L_{6}

Figure 2:
The
z
-plane is divided into six analytical domains
S_{n},n=1,...,6
by
six rays
L_{n}
.
\varkappa_{n}
and
\kappa_{n}
are spectral singularities.

It was shown that the eigenfunction
\mu(z)
defined by (
2.22
) has the following properties
[
13
]
.

Proposition 1
.

The equations (
2.24
) uniquely define a
3\times 3
-matrix valued solutions
\mu(z)\triangleq\mu(z;x,t)
of (
2.23
)
with the following properties:

(1)

det
\mu(z)
=1;

(2)

\mu(z)
is piecewise meromorphic with respect to
\Sigma
, as function of the spectral parameter
z
;

(3)

\mu(z)
obeys the symmetries
\mu(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{\mu(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{\mu(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{\mu(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}
and
\mu(z)=\overline{\mu(\bar{z}^{-1})}
, where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),\quad\Gamma_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&0\end{array}\right),\quad\Gamma_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{array}\right);

(4)

\mu(z)
has pole singularities at
\varkappa_{n}=e^{\frac{n\pi i}{3}}
with
\mu=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{z-\varkappa_{n}})
as
z\to\varkappa_{n}
;

(5)

\mu(z)\to I
as
z\to\infty
, and for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
,
\mu(z)
is bounded as
x\to-\infty
and
\mu(z)\to I
as
x\to+\infty
;

Denote
\mu_{\pm}(z;x,t)
as the limiting values of
\mu(z^{\prime};x,t)
as
z^{\prime}\to z
from the
positive or negative side of
L_{n}
, then they are related as follows

\displaystyle\mu_{+}(z)=\mu_{-}(z)e^{Q}v_{n}e^{-Q},\quad z\in L_{n},\ n=1,\cdots,6,

(2.37)

where
v_{n}
only depends on
z
and is completely determined by
u(x,0)
, i.e., by the initial data for the Cauchy problem (
1.8
). Take
L_{1}=\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
L_{4}=\mathbb{R}^{-}
as an example,
v_{n}
for
n=1,4
has a special matrix structure

\displaystyle v_{n}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-r_{\pm}(z)&0\\
\bar{r}_{\pm}(z)&1-|r_{\pm}(z)|^{2}&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}.

r_{\pm}(z)
are single scalar
functions, with
r_{\pm}(z)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\pm})
, and
r_{\pm}(z)=\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})
as
z\to\pm\infty
. The symmetry of
\mu(z)
gives that
r_{\pm}(z)=\overline{r_{\pm}(z^{-1})}
,
therefore it also has
r_{\pm}(z)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\pm})
and
\lim_{z\to 0^{\pm}}r(z)_{\pm}=0
.
Moreover, the singularities at
\pm 1
give that
r_{\pm}(\pm 1)=0
. So we may define
reflection coefficient

\displaystyle r(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r_{\pm}(z),&z\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm},\\[12.0pt]
0,&z=0.\\
\end{array}\right.

Then
r\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
r(z)=\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})
as
z\to\infty
. In reference
[
13
,
49
]
, it was shown that there exist at most a finite
number of simple poles
z_{n}
of
\mu(z)
lying in
S_{1}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C};\ |z|>1\}
and
w_{m}
lying in
S_{1}\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C};\ |z|=1\}
.
And there are no poles except
\pm 1
,
\pm\omega
and
\pm\omega^{2}
on the contour
\Sigma
. Note that, unlike the case of
2\times 2
matrix function, the residue conditions have two special matrix forms with only one nonzero entry.

To differentiate this two types of poles, we denote them as
z_{n}
,
z_{n}^{A}
and
w_{m}
,
w_{m}^{A}
, respectively.
Denote
N_{1}
,
N_{1}^{A}
,
N_{2}
and
N_{2}^{A}
as the number of
z_{n}
,
z_{n}^{A}
,
w_{m}
, and
w_{m}^{A}
, respectively. The symmetries of
\mu
imply
\bar{z}_{n}^{-1}
and
\frac{1}{\bar{z}_{n}^{A}}
are also the poles of
\mu
in
S_{1}
. It is convenient to define
\zeta_{n}=z_{n}
, and
\zeta_{n+N_{1}}=\bar{z}_{n}^{-1}
for
n=1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,N_{1}
;
\zeta_{m+2N_{1}}=w_{m}
for
m=1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,N_{2}
;
\zeta_{j+2N_{1}+N_{2}}=z_{j}^{A}
, and
\zeta_{j+2N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{1}^{A}}=\frac{1}{\bar{z}_{n}^{A}}
for
j=1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,N_{1}^{A}
;
\zeta_{m+2N_{1}+2N_{1}^{A}+N_{2}}=w_{l}^{A}
for
l=1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,N_{2}^{A}
. For the sake of brevity, let

\displaystyle N_{0}=2N_{1}+2N_{1}^{A}+N_{2}+N_{2}^{A}.

Moreover,
\omega\zeta_{n}
,
\omega^{2}\zeta_{n}
,
\bar{\zeta}_{n}
,
\omega\bar{\zeta}_{n}
,
\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{n}
are also poles of
\mu(z)
in
S_{j}
,
j=2,\cdots,6
. So for convenience, let
\zeta_{n+N_{0}}=\omega\bar{\zeta}_{n}
,
\zeta_{n+2N_{0}}=\omega\zeta_{n},

\zeta_{n+3N_{0}}=\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{n}
,
\zeta_{n+4N_{0}}=\omega^{2}\zeta_{n}
and
\zeta_{n+5N_{0}}=\bar{\zeta}_{n}
for
n=1,...,N_{0}
.
Therefore, the discrete spectrum is

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\zeta_{n}\right\}_{n=1}^{6N_{0}},

(2.43)

with
\zeta_{n}\in S_{1}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{n}\in S_{6}
. And the distribution of
\mathcal{Z}
on the
z
-plane is shown in Figure
3
.

L_{1}
L_{2}
L_{3}
L_{4}
L_{6}
L_{5}
\omega\bar{w}_{m}
\omega^{2}w_{m}
\frac{\omega}{z_{n}}
\frac{\omega^{2}}{\bar{z}_{n}}
\frac{\omega}{\bar{z}_{n}}
\frac{\omega^{2}}{z_{n}}
z_{n}
\bar{z}_{n}
\omega\bar{z}_{n}
\omega^{2}z_{n}
\frac{1}{\bar{z}_{n}}
\frac{1}{z_{n}}
\omega z_{n}
\omega^{2}\bar{z}_{n}
w_{m}
\bar{w}_{m}
\omega w_{m}
\omega^{2}\bar{w}_{m}

Figure 3:
Distribution of the discrete spectrum
\mathcal{Z}
in the
z
-plane.

Besides, the poles of
\mu(z)
on
L_{n}
,
n=1,...,6
may occur and they correspond to spectral singularities. Therefore, it is reasonable to give the following assumption:
As shown in
[
13
]
, denote
norming constant

c_{n}
. Therefore, there are residue conditions as

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}\mu(z)=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}\mu(z)e^{Q}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-c_{n}&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)e^{-Q},

for
n=1,...,2N_{1}+N_{2}
and

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{j}}\mu(z)=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{j}}\mu(z)e^{Q}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&-c_{j+2N_{1}+N_{2}}\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)e^{-Q},

for
j=1+2N_{1}+N_{2},...,N_{0}
.
In addition, the collection

\sigma_{d}=\left\{\zeta_{n},C_{n}\right\}^{6N_{0}}_{n=1}

is called the
scattering data
with
C_{n}=c_{n}
,
C_{n+N_{0}}=\omega\bar{c}_{n}
,
C_{n+2N_{0}}=\omega c_{n},

C_{n+3N_{0}}=\omega^{2}\bar{c}_{n}
,
C_{n+4N_{0}}=\omega^{2}c_{n}
and
C_{n+5N_{0}}=\bar{c}_{n}
for
n=1,...,N_{0}
.

To deal with our following work,
we assume our initial data satisfies that
u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
to generate generic scattering data such that
\mu(z)
has no the poles
on
L_{n}\setminus\{\varkappa_{n}\}
,
n=1,...,6
and at point
z=e^{\frac{\pi i}{6}}
. And also
r(z)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
This statement will be given is section
2.3
.

2.2
Set up of RH problem

We replace
the pair of variables
(x,t)
by
(y,t)
with
y
defined in (
2.21
),
The price to pay for this is that the solution of the initial problem can be given only implicitly,
or parametrically. It will be given in terms of functions in the new scale, whereas the original scale will also be given in terms of functions in the new scale.
By the definition of the new scale
y(x,t)
, we denote the phase function

\theta_{jl}(z)=-i\left[\xi(\lambda_{j}(z)-\lambda_{l}(z))+\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}(z)}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{l}(z)}\right)\right],\quad\xi=\frac{y}{t}.

(2.50)

Especially,

\theta_{12}(z)=\sqrt{3}\left(z-\frac{1}{z}\right)\left[\xi-\frac{1}{z^{2}-1+z^{-2}}\right],\quad

(2.51)

with
\theta_{23}(z)=\theta_{12}(\omega z),\text{ and }\theta_{31}(z)=\theta_{12}(\omega^{2}z)
. Furthermore, denote
\theta_{12}(\zeta_{n})\triangleq[\theta_{12}]_{n}
.
Let

M(z;y,t)\triangleq\mu(z;x(y,t),t).

(2.52)

which solves the following RH problem.

RHP 1
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M(z)\triangleq M(z;y,t)
which satisfies:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M(z)
is meromorphic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
and has single poles;

\blacktriangleright
Symmetry:
M(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{M(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}

and
M(z)=\overline{M(\bar{z}^{-1})}
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
M
has continuous boundary values
M_{\pm}(z)
on
L_{n}

M_{+}(z)=M_{-}(z)V_{n}(z),\hskip 8.5359ptz\in L_{n},

(2.53)

where

\displaystyle V_{1}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-r(z)e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
\bar{r}(z)e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1-|r(z)|^{2}&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle V_{2}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&-r(\omega z)e^{it\theta_{23}}\\
0&\bar{r}(\omega z)e^{-it\theta_{23}}&1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle V_{3}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}&0&\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z)e^{it\theta_{13}}\\
0&1&0\\
-r(\omega^{2}z)e^{-it\theta_{13}}&0&1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle V_{4}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-r(z)e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
\bar{r}(z)e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1-|r(z)|^{2}&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle V_{5}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&-r(\omega z)e^{it\theta_{23}}\\
0&\bar{r}(\omega z)e^{-it\theta_{23}}&1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle V_{6}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}&0&\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z)e^{it\theta_{13}}\\
0&1&0\\
-r(\omega^{2}z)e^{-it\theta_{13}}&0&1\end{array}\right)

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M(z)=I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty;

(2.72)

\blacktriangleright
Singularities: As
z\to\varkappa_{n}=e^{\frac{i\pi(n-1)}{3}}
,
n=1,...,6
,
the limit of
M(z)
has pole singularities

\displaystyle M(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp 1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\alpha_{\pm}&\alpha_{\pm}&\beta_{\pm}\\
-\alpha_{\pm}&-\alpha_{\pm}&-\beta_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm 1,

\displaystyle M(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
\beta_{\pm}&\alpha_{\pm}&\alpha_{\pm}\\
-\beta_{\pm}&-\alpha_{\pm}&-\alpha_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega^{2},

\displaystyle M(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\alpha_{\pm}&-\beta_{\pm}&-\alpha_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\\
\alpha_{\pm}&\beta_{\pm}&\alpha_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega,

with
\alpha_{\pm}=\alpha_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\alpha}_{\pm}
,
\beta_{\pm}=\beta_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\beta}_{\pm}
and
M^{-1}
has same specific
matrix structure with
\alpha_{\pm}
,
\beta_{\pm}
replaced by
\tilde{\alpha}_{\pm}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{\pm}
. Moreover,
\left(\alpha_{\pm},\ \beta_{\pm}\right)\neq 0
iff
\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{\pm},\ \tilde{\beta}_{\pm}\right)\neq 0
;

\blacktriangleright
Residue conditions:
M(z)
has simple poles at each point in
\mathcal{Z}\cup\bar{\mathcal{Z}}
with:

for
n=1,...,2N_{1}+N_{2}

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-c_{n}e^{it[\theta_{12}]_{n}}&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega\bar{\zeta}_{n}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega\bar{\zeta}_{n}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&-\omega\bar{c}_{n}e^{it\theta_{13}(\omega\bar{\zeta}_{n})}\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega\zeta_{n}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega\zeta_{n}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
-\omega c_{n}e^{-it\theta_{13}(\omega\zeta_{n})}&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+2N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{n}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{n}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&-\omega^{2}\bar{c}_{n}e^{-it\theta_{23}(\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{n})}&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+3N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega^{2}\zeta_{n}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega^{2}\zeta_{n}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&-\omega^{2}c_{n}e^{it\theta_{23}(\omega^{2}\zeta_{n})}\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+4N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\bar{\zeta}_{n}}M=\lim_{z\to\bar{\zeta}_{n}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
-\bar{c}_{n}e^{-it[\theta_{12}]_{n}}&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+5N_{0}},

and for
j=1+2N_{1}+N_{2},...,2N_{1}^{A}+N_{2}^{A}+2N_{1}+N_{2}
,

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega\zeta_{j}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega\zeta_{j}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-\omega c_{j}e^{it\theta_{12}(\omega\zeta_{j})}&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+2N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\bar{\zeta}_{j}}M=\lim_{z\to\bar{\zeta}_{j}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&-\bar{c}_{j}e^{it\theta_{13}(\bar{\zeta}_{j})}\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+5N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega^{2}\zeta_{j}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega^{2}\zeta_{j}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
-\omega^{2}c_{j}e^{-it\theta_{13}(\omega^{2}\zeta_{j})}&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+4N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega\bar{\zeta}_{j}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega\bar{\zeta}_{j}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&-\omega\bar{c}_{j}e^{-it\theta_{23}(\omega\bar{\zeta}_{j})}&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+N_{0}},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{j}}M=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{j}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&-c_{j}e^{it\theta_{23}(\zeta_{j})}\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n},

\displaystyle\res_{z=\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{j}}M=\lim_{z\to\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{j}}M\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
-\omega^{2}\bar{c}_{j}e^{-it\theta_{12}(\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{j})}&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)\triangleq\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}MB_{n+3N_{0}}.

Denote
M(z;y,t)=(M_{jl}(z;y,t))_{jl=1}^{3}
,
then solution of Novikov equation (
1.8
) can be obtained by the following reconstruction formula

\displaystyle u(x,t)=u(y(x,t),t)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tilde{m}_{1}(y,t)\left(\frac{M_{33}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}{M_{11}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{m}_{3}(y,t)\left(\frac{M_{33}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}{M_{11}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}\right)^{-1/2}-1,

(2.118)

where

x(y,t)=y+c_{+}(x,t)=y+\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{M_{33}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}{M_{11}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)},

(2.119)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{m}_{l}\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{3}M_{jl}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t),\ l=1,2,3.

2.3
Scattering maps

In this section, our purpose is to demonstrate the following proposition about reflection coefficient
r(z)
.

Proposition 2
.

If the initial data
u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, then
r(z)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. In addition, there exist a fixed positive constant
C_{1,r}
and a fixed constant
C_{2,r}
satisfying that if
u_{0}-u_{0,xx}>C_{2,r}>-1
,
C_{1,r}>\parallel u_{0}-u_{0,xx}\parallel_{L^{1}}
and
C_{1,r}>\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{j,3}}
,
j=1,\infty
, then
|r(z)|<1
for
z\in\mathbb{R}
.

The fact that
r(z)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
is obviously. In this Section, we will give that
|r(\pm 1)|=0
and
r(z)
is continuous near
z=\pm 1
. So, it is reasonable to assume that there a fixed small sufficiently positive constant
\epsilon
satisfying:

1.
(z-1)\mu_{\pm}(z)\neq 0
for
z
in
(1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)
;

2. for
z\in(1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)
,
|r(z)|<\frac{1}{2}
.

Since
\mu_{\pm}
are two fundamental matrix solutions of the Lax pair (
2.23
), there exists a linear relation between
\mu_{+}
and
\mu_{-}
, namely

\mu_{+}(z;x,t)=\mu_{-}(z;x,t)S(z),

(2.120)

where
S(z)
is called scattering matrix and only depended on
z
.
Take
x\to-\infty
, then we obtain the integral representation of
S(z)
as:

S(z)=I-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{-\hat{\Lambda}(z)\int_{-\infty}^{x}(q^{2}(v,t)-1)dv}[U\mu_{+}(x,t;z)]ds.

(2.121)

The entries of
S(z)
are defined and continuous for
z
in

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}S_{1}\cup S_{2}&\mathbb{R}^{+}&\omega\mathbb{R}^{+}\\
\mathbb{R}^{+}&S_{3}\cup S_{4}&\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}^{+}\\
\omega\mathbb{R}^{+}&\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}^{+}&S_{5}\cup S_{6}\end{array}\right)\setminus\{\pm 1,\pm\omega,\pm\omega^{2}\}.

Further by symmetries,
S(z)
can be written as

\displaystyle S(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}s_{11}(z)&s_{12}(z)&\overline{s_{12}(\omega\bar{z})}\\
\overline{s_{12}(\bar{z})}&\overline{s_{11}(\bar{z})}&s_{12}(\omega z)\\
s_{12}(\omega^{2}z)&\overline{s_{12}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}&\overline{s_{11}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\end{array}\right),\ s_{11}(z)=\overline{s_{11}(\omega\bar{z})}.

Rewrite (
2.120
) as

S(z)=\mu_{-}(z;x,t)^{-1}\mu_{+}(z;x,t).

Note that
\mu_{\pm}(z;x,t)
has same asymptotic behavior (
1
)-(
1
), we see that

\pm 1
,
\pm\omega
,
\pm\omega^{2}
are not the singularity of
S(z)
any more. Then when the initial data
u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
r(z)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
too. In addition,
S(\varkappa_{j})\sim I
, for
j=1,...,6
. It gives that
r(\pm 1)=0
.
Furthermore,

\det S(z)=1,\ \ \ S(z)\sim I\ as\ z\to\infty.

And the reflection coefficient can be represented as
r(z)=\frac{s_{11}(z)}{s_{12}(z)}
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

S^{A}(z)=[s_{jl}^{A}(z)]_{j,l=1}^{3}=(S(z)^{-1})^{T}
is the cofactor matrix of
S(z)
, with integral representation

S^{A}(z)=I+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{\int_{-\hat{\Lambda}(z)\infty}^{x}q^{2}(v,t)-1dv}[U^{T}\mu_{+}^{A}(x,t;z)]ds.

(2.125)

The entries of it are defined and continuous for
z
in

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}S_{4}\cup S_{5}&\mathbb{R}^{-}&\omega\mathbb{R}^{-}\\
\mathbb{R}^{-}&S_{1}\cup S_{6}&\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}^{-}\\
\omega\mathbb{R}^{-}&\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}^{-}&S_{2}\cup S_{3}\end{array}\right)\setminus\{\pm 1,\pm\omega,\pm\omega^{2}\}.

It has same symmetry as
S(z)
and
s_{11}^{A}(z)=\overline{s_{11}^{A}(\omega\bar{z})}
. And it also does not have singularity at
\pm 1,\ \pm\omega,\ \pm\omega^{2}
.
Similarly, for
z\in\mathbb{R}
, the reflection coefficient can be represented as
r(z)=\frac{s_{11}^{A}(z)}{s_{12}^{A}(z)}
.

Now we begin to demonstrate Proposition
2
. We only consider the
x
-part of Lax pair to give the proof of proposition
2
in this section. In fact, taking account of
t
-part of Lax pair and through the standard direct scattering transform, it can be deduced that
r(z)
have linear time evolution:
r(z,t)=e^{\frac{i}{4\lambda^{2}(z)}(z-\frac{1}{z})}r(z,0)
. Recall that
r(z)
has different representation on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\mathbb{R}^{-}
. In this section, we only consider the
L^{2}
-integrability and the boundedness of
r(z)
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. The case of
z\in\mathbb{R}^{-}
can be obtain through same analysis.
Because of the singularity on
1
of the spectral problem (
2.23
), we divide our approach into three cases:

(0,1-\epsilon),\ (1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon),\ (1+\epsilon,+\infty).

Remark: If
(s_{0},s_{1})=0
, the analysis will be more simple. The property near
z=1
do not need to be considered separately.

For
z>0
,
z
away from
1
, recall
r(z)=\frac{s_{12}(z)}{s_{11}(z)}
. So,
the fact that the
L^{\infty}
-norm of
r(z)
for
z
in
(1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)
is controlled by
u_{0}
is equivalent to the following two proposition:
the maximal value, the minimal value of
s_{11}(z)
and the maximal value
s_{12}(z)
for
z
in
(1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)
is controlled by
u_{0}

From (
2.121
), we give the integral equations of
s_{12}(z)
and
s_{11}(z)
:

\displaystyle s_{11}(z)=1-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{3}U_{1k}(y,z)[\mu_{+}]_{k1}(y,z)dy,

(2.126)

\displaystyle s_{12}(z)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(z-1/z)p(y)}\sum_{k=1}^{3}U_{1k}(y,z)[\mu_{+}]_{k2}(y,z)dy.

(2.127)

Here,
U_{ij}(y,z)
is the entire of (
2.16
),
p(y)=y-\int_{y}^{+\infty}\left(q^{2}(s,0)-1\right)ds
. It has special structure as
U_{ij}(y,z)=h_{ij}(z)c_{2}(y)+g_{ij}(z)c_{1}(y)
, with
c_{1}(y)=\frac{q_{x}}{q}(y,0)
,
c_{2}(y)=q^{-2}(y,0)-q^{2}(y,0)
and

\displaystyle h_{ij}(z)=\frac{\lambda_{j}(z)}{3\lambda_{i}(z)^{2}-1},\ g_{ij}(z)=\frac{\lambda_{j}(z)\lambda_{i}(z)-\lambda_{j}(z)^{2}}{3\lambda_{i}(z)^{2}-1}.

(2.128)

Lemma 1
.

For
u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we have that

\displaystyle\parallel c_{1}(y)\parallel_{1}\leq

\displaystyle(1+C_{r,2})^{-1}\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{1,3}},

(2.129)

\displaystyle\parallel c_{2}(y)\parallel_{1}\leq

\displaystyle(1+C_{r,2})^{-2/3}\left(1+(1+2\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{\infty,3}})^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\left(1+(1+2\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{\infty,3}})^{2/3}\right)\parallel u_{0}-[u_{0}]_{xx}\parallel_{1}.

(2.130)

Proof.

Denote
q(y,0)\triangleq q_{0}(y)
. When
u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
with
u_{0}-[u_{0}]_{xx}>C_{r,2}
,

\displaystyle(1+C_{r,2})^{1/3}\leq|q_{0}(y)|\leq 1+2\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{\infty,3}},\ \parallel q_{0}^{\prime}(y)\parallel_{1}\leq(1+C_{r,2})^{-2/3}\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{1,3}},

\displaystyle\parallel 1-q_{0}^{4}\parallel_{1}\leq\left(1+(1+2\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{\infty,3}})^{1/3}\right)\left(1+(1+2\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{\infty,3}})^{2/3}\right)\parallel u_{0}-[u_{0}]_{xx}\parallel_{1}.

Here we use the fact:

\displaystyle|1-q_{0}|=\frac{|u_{0}-[u_{0}]_{xx}|}{1+q_{0}+q_{0}^{2}}\leq|u_{0}-[u_{0}]_{xx}|.

Thus it is easily to obtain the result.
∎

The following property comes soon by simple calculation:

Lemma 2
.

For
i,j=1,2,3
,
h_{ij}(z)
and
g_{ij}(z)
belong in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{z;\ |z-1|<\epsilon\})
,
\infty\geq p>1
.
h_{ij}(z),\ g_{ij}(z)\sim\frac{1}{z-1}
when
i\neq 3
as
z\to 1
.
h_{ij}(z)\sim\frac{1}{z}
,
g_{ij}(z)\sim 1
as
z\to\infty
, and
h_{ij}(z)\sim z
,
g_{ij}(z)\sim 1
as
z\to 0
.

We denote the positive constant

C(h,g,\epsilon)
relying on
h_{ij}(z),\ g_{ij}(z)
and
\epsilon
with

C(h,g,\epsilon)=\underset{i,j=1,2,3}{\max}\left\{\parallel h_{ij}\parallel_{L^{\infty}\left((1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)\right)},\ \parallel g_{ij}\parallel_{L^{\infty}\left((1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)\right)}\right\}.

Then by (
2.126
)-(
2.127
), it requires us to research the property of
\mu_{+}
in following subsections.

However unlike the focusing NLS equation
[
31
]
, the Novikov equation admits a
3\times 3
matrix Lax pair,

1-|r(z)|^{2}\geq 0
does not always hold.
In fact, from the proof in following Subsection, we can ensure
|r(z)|<1
for
z
not near
1
, because its
\infty
-norm can be controlled by initial value.
And as we assuming previous,
s_{11}^{A}(\omega^{2}z)\neq 0
on
l_{1}=\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{1\}
, which implies
s_{11}^{A}(\omega^{2}z)=|s_{11}(z)|^{2}(1-|r(z)|^{2})\neq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{1\}
. So
1-|r(z)|^{2}\neq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
except
z=1
, and it does note change sign by its continuity. Thus,
|r(z)|<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Similarly,
|r(z)|<1
on
\mathbb{R}^{-}
.

First, our goal is to prove that
L^{\infty}
-norm of
\mu^{+}(z)
for
z
in
(1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)
is controlled by
u_{0}
. Here, We give the details of
z\in(1+\epsilon,+\infty)
. The case
z\in(0,1-\epsilon)
can be demonstrated following the same way. Recall the integral equation (
2.24
) and set
t=0
,
we only consider its first column
\mu^{+}_{1}(z)
, the other columns can be obtained analogously.

Introduce the integral operator
T_{+}
:

\displaystyle T_{+}(f)(x,z)=\int_{x}^{+\infty}K_{+}(x,y,z)f(y,z)dy,

(2.131)

where integral kernel
K_{+}(x,y,z)=\left([K_{+}(x,y,z)]_{ij}\right)_{3\times 3}
is a
3\times 3
matrix with entire

\displaystyle[K_{+}(x,y,z)]_{ij}=e^{(\lambda_{i}(z)-\lambda_{1}(z))(p(x)-p(y))}U_{ij}(y,z).

(2.132)

Here,
U_{ij}(y,z)
is the entire of (
2.16
) with
t=0
. And we denote
e_{1}=(1,0,0)^{T}
,
then (
2.23
) trans to

\displaystyle[\mu_{+}]_{1}=e_{1}+T_{+}([\mu_{+}]_{1}).

(2.133)

Lemma 3
.

T_{+}
is a integral operator defined above, then it is a bounded operator on
L^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\times(1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)\right)
. And its norm is under control by
u_{0}
.

Proof.

For any function
f(x,z)=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})^{T}
in
L^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\times(1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)\right)
,
by the definition (
2.131
), we have

\displaystyle T_{+}(f)(x,z)=

\displaystyle\int_{x}^{+\infty}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sum_{j=1}^{3}U_{1j}f_{j}\\
\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3}U_{2j}f_{j}\right)e^{(\lambda_{2}(z)-\lambda_{1}(z))(p(x)-p(y))}\\
\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3}U_{3j}f_{j}\right)e^{(\lambda_{3}(z)-\lambda_{1}(z))(p(x)-p(y))}\end{array}\right)dy.

It immediately derive that

\displaystyle|T_{+}(f)(x,z)|\lesssim

\displaystyle C(h,g,\epsilon)\left(\int_{x}^{+\infty}|c_{2}(y)|dy+\int_{x}^{+\infty}|c_{1}(y)|dy\right)\parallel f\parallel_{\infty}.

(2.137)

So
T_{+}\in\mathcal{B}\left(L^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\times(1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)\right)\right),
with

\parallel T_{+}\parallel\leq C(h,g,\epsilon)\left(\int_{x}^{+\infty}|c_{2}(y)|dy+\int_{x}^{+\infty}|c_{1}(y)|dy\right).

∎

Consider the Volterra operator
T_{+}^{n}
with

\displaystyle T_{+}^{n}(f)(x,z)=T_{+}\left(T_{+}^{n-1}(f)\right)(x,z)

(2.138)

Denote
K^{n}_{+}
is the integral kernel of
[T_{+}]^{n}
as

\displaystyle K^{n}_{+}(x,y,z)=\int_{x}^{y}\int_{x}^{y_{n-1}}...\int_{x}^{y_{2}}K_{+}(x,y_{1},z)K_{+}(y_{1},y_{2},z)...K_{+}(y_{n-1},y,z)dy_{1}...dy_{n-1},

with

\displaystyle|K^{n}_{+}(x,y,z)|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(h,g,\epsilon)^{n}}{(n-1)!}\left(|c_{1}(y)|+|c_{2}(y)|\right)

\displaystyle\left[\left(\int_{x}^{+\infty}|c_{2}(y)|dy\right)^{n-1}+\left(\int_{x}^{+\infty}|c_{1}(y)|dy\right)^{n-1}\right].

(2.139)

Then the standard Volterra theory gives the of the operator
(I-T_{+})^{-1}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}T^{n}
with following operator norm:

\displaystyle\parallel(I-T_{+})^{-1}\parallel\leq e^{C(h,g,\epsilon)\left(\parallel c_{1}(y)\parallel_{L^{1}}+\parallel c_{2}(y)\parallel_{L^{1}}\right)}.

(2.140)

The equations (
2.133
) are solvable with:

\displaystyle\mu^{+}_{1}(x,z)=(I-T_{+})^{-1}(T_{+}(e_{1}))(x,z).

(2.141)

It leads to the boundedness of
[\mu_{+}]_{1}(x,z)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\big((1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)\big)
with

\displaystyle\parallel\mu^{+}_{1}(x,z)\parallel_{L^{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle e^{C(h,g,\epsilon)\left(\parallel c_{1}(y)\parallel_{L^{1}}+\parallel c_{2}(y)\parallel_{L^{1}}\right)}C(h,g,\epsilon)\left(\parallel c_{1}(y)\parallel_{L^{1}}+\parallel c_{2}(y)\parallel_{L^{1}}\right).

(2.142)

Then we begin to estimate
s_{12}(z)
and
s_{11}(z)
. (
2.126
) and (
2.127
) give that for
z\in(1+\epsilon,+\infty)\cup(0,1-\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle|s_{11}(z)-1|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{3}\parallel U_{1k}\parallel_{1}\parallel[\mu_{+}]_{k1}\parallel_{\infty},

(2.143)

\displaystyle|s_{12}(z)|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{3}\parallel U_{1k}\parallel_{1}\parallel[\mu_{+}]_{k2}\parallel_{\infty}.

(2.144)

So the
\infty
-norm of
|s_{11}(z)-1|
and
|s_{12}(z)|
are controlled by the initial value. And when
\sum_{k=1}^{3}\parallel U_{1k}\parallel_{1}\parallel[\mu_{+}]_{k1}\parallel_{\infty}
small enough,
we deduce that
2>s_{11}(z)>C(u_{0})>0
with a positive constant
C(u_{0})
depending on
u_{0}
. Thus,

\displaystyle|r(z)|=

\displaystyle|\frac{b(z)}{a(z)}|<\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{3}\parallel U_{1k}\parallel_{1}\parallel[\mu_{+}]_{k2}\parallel_{\infty}}{C(u_{0})}.

(2.145)

Together with (
2.142
), Lemma
1
and Lemma
2
,
|r(z)|
is proportional to
\parallel c_{1}\parallel_{1}
and
\parallel c_{2}\parallel_{1}
, namely
\parallel u_{0}-[u_{0}]_{xx}\parallel_{1}
,
\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{\infty,3}}
and
\parallel u_{0}\parallel_{W^{1,3}}
. Hence, there exists a positive constant
C_{r,1}
to control above three norm of
u_{0}
and admit that
|r(z)|<1
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus(1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon).

Thus when initial datum
u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
s_{11}(z)
and
s_{12}(z)
are continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus(1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)
. In fact, take
\epsilon\to 0
, then we obtain that
s_{11}(z)
and
s_{12}(z)
are continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{1\}
. Moreover, as we assuming preceding,
s_{11}(z)\neq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. So
r(z)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{1\}
. Then the asymptotic property:
r(z)\to 0
as
z\to 1
in
\mathbb{R}^{+}
gives that
z=1
is a removable singular point of
r(z)
.

3
Normalization of the RH problem

3.1
Stationary phase points and decay domains

The long-time asymptotic of the RHP
1
is affected by the growth and decay of the exponential function
e^{\pm 2it\theta_{jl}}
with

\displaystyle\theta_{12}(z)=\sqrt{3}\left(z-\frac{1}{z}\right)\left[\xi-\frac{1}{z^{2}-1+z^{-2}}\right],

(3.1)

\displaystyle\theta_{13}(z)=-\theta_{12}(\omega^{2}z),\ \theta_{23}(z)=\theta_{12}(\omega z).

(3.2)

which is appearing in both the jump relation and the residue conditions. So we need control the real part of the phase functions
\pm 2it\theta_{jl}
.
In fact, from (
3.2
), we only need consider the phase points and signature tables of
\theta_{12}
. Therefore, in this section, we introduce a new transform
M(z)\to M^{(1)}(z)
, which make that the
M^{(1)}(z)
is well behaved as
t\to\infty
along any characteristic line.
For the oscillatory term
e^{2it\theta_{12}}
as
t\to\infty
, we consider the real stationary points of
\theta_{12}(z)
on
\mathbb{R}=L_{1}\cup L_{4}
. Let

\xi=\frac{y}{t},\ \ \breve{k}=z-\frac{1}{z},

then

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\theta_{12}}{\partial z}=\frac{\partial\theta_{12}}{\partial\breve{k}}\frac{\partial\breve{k}}{\partial z}=\sqrt{3}\left(\xi-\frac{1-\breve{k}^{2}}{(\breve{k}^{2}+1)^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right).

(3.3)

Therefore the stationary points satisfy the equation

\displaystyle\xi(\breve{k}^{2})^{2}+(2\xi+1)\breve{k}^{2}+\xi-1=0,

(3.4)

from which, we found that

Case I:

For the domains
\xi<-{1}/{8}
and
\xi>1
, there is no stationary point on
\mathbb{R}
;

Case II:

For the domain
-{1}/{8}<\xi<0
, on
\mathbb{R}
there are 8 stationary points, which reorder as

\xi_{1}>\xi_{2}>\xi_{3}>\xi_{4}>\xi_{5}>\xi_{6}>\xi_{7}>\xi_{8},

with
\xi_{1}=1/\xi_{4}=-1/\xi_{5}=-\xi_{8}
and
\xi_{2}=1/\xi_{3}=-1/\xi_{6}=-\xi_{7}
;

Case III:

For the domain
0\leq\xi<1
, on
\mathbb{R}
there are 4 stationary points, which reorder as

\xi_{1}>\xi_{2}>\xi_{3}>\xi_{4},

with
\xi_{1}=1/\xi_{2}=-1/\xi_{3}=-\xi_{4}
;

Next we consider the signature table of
\text{Im}\theta_{12}
, which is determined by
the real part of
it\theta_{12}

\displaystyle\text{Re}(it\theta_{12})=-\sqrt{3}t\text{Im}\theta_{12}=-t\text{Im}z\left(1+|z|^{-2}\right)\xi+

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sqrt{3}t\text{Im}z\left(1+|z|^{-2}\right)\left(-|z|^{6}-|z|^{4}+4\text{Re}^{2}z|z|^{2}-|z|^{2}\right)}{|z|^{8}+1+2[(\text{Re}^{2}z-\text{Im}^{2}z)^{2}-4\text{Re}^{2}z\text{Im}^{2}z]-2(1+|z|^{4})(\text{Re}^{2}z-\text{Im}^{2}z)+|z|^{4}}.

(3.5)

For the above case I, the signature table of
\text{Im}\theta_{12}(z)
are shown by (a) and (d) in Figure
4
;
For the above Case II and Case III, the signature tables of
\text{Im}\theta_{12}(z)

are shown by (b) and (c) in Figure
4
, respectively.

\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle p(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,&\text{as }\xi>1\text{ and }\xi<-\frac{1}{8},\\[10.0pt]

3.2
), if
\xi_{j},j=1,...,p(\xi)
is phase points on the real axis
\mathbb{R}
,
then
\omega\xi_{j}
and
\omega^{2}\xi_{j}
for
j=1,...,p(\xi)
are also the stationary phase points on
\omega\mathbb{R}
and
\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}
respectively.

\xi_{n,j}:=\omega^{n}\xi_{j}
denoting the
jth
phase point on the
\omega^{n}\mathbb{R}
with

.

and 24 phase points

-\frac{1}{8}<\xi<0
, respectively. See Figure
5
.

\mathbb{R}

\xi_{0}=-\infty
,
\xi_{p(\xi)+1}=+\infty
, and introduce some intervals when
j=1,...,p(\xi)
, for
0\leq\xi<1

\displaystyle I_{j1}=I_{j2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j+1}}{2},\xi_{j}\right),&j\text{ is odd number},\\[10.0pt]
\left(\xi_{j},\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j-1}}{2}\right),&j\text{ is even number},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle I_{j3}=I_{j4}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\xi_{j},\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j-1}}{2}\right),&j\text{ is odd number},\\[10.0pt]
\left(\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j+1}}{2},\xi_{j}\right),&j\text{ is even number},\end{array}\right.

and for
-{1}/{8}<\xi<0
,

\displaystyle I_{j1}=I_{j2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\xi_{j},\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j-1}}{2}\right),&j\text{ is odd number},\\[10.0pt]
\left(\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j+1}}{2},\xi_{j}\right),&j\text{ is even number},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle I_{j3}=I_{j4}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j+1}}{2},\xi_{j}\right),&j\text{ is odd number},\\[10.0pt]
\left(\xi_{j},\frac{\xi_{j}+\xi_{j-1}}{2}\right),&j\text{ is even number},\end{array}\right..

3.2
Conjugation

For convenience, we denote

\mathcal{N}\triangleq\left\{1,...,N_{0}\right\},\ \tilde{\mathcal{N}}\triangleq\left\{1,...,2N_{1}+N_{2}\right\},\ \tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{A}\triangleq\left\{1+2N_{1}+N_{2},...,N_{0}\right\}.

Further to distinguish different type of zeros, we introduce a small positive constant
\delta_{0}
to give the partitions
\Delta,\nabla
and
\lozenge
of
\mathcal{N}
as follow

\displaystyle\nabla_{1}=\left\{j\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}};\text{Im}\theta_{12}(\zeta_{j})>\delta_{0}\right\},\Delta_{1}=\left\{j\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}};\text{Im}\theta_{12}(\zeta_{j})<0\right\},

\displaystyle\nabla_{2}=\left\{i\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{A};\text{Im}\theta_{23}(\zeta_{i})>\delta_{0}\right\},\Delta_{2}=\left\{i\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{A};\text{Im}\theta_{23}(\zeta_{i})<0\right\},

(3.17)

\displaystyle\lozenge_{1}=\left\{j_{0}\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}};0\leq\text{Im}\theta_{12}(\zeta_{j_{0}})\leq\delta_{0}\right\},

\displaystyle\lozenge_{2}=\left\{i_{0}\in\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{A};0\leq\text{Im}\theta_{23}(\zeta_{i_{0}})\leq\delta_{0}\right\},

(3.18)

\displaystyle\nabla=\nabla_{1}\cup\nabla_{2},\Delta=\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2},

\displaystyle\lozenge=\{n\in\mathcal{N};\exists k\in\{1,...,5\},n-kN_{0}\in\lozenge_{1}\cup\lozenge_{2}\}.

(3.19)

For
\zeta_{n}
with
n\in\Delta
, the residue of
M(z)
at
\zeta_{n}
in (
1
) and (
1
) grows without bound as
t\to\infty
. Similarly, for
\zeta_{n}
with
n\in\nabla
, the residue are approaching to
0
. Denote two constants
\mathcal{N}(\lozenge)=|\lozenge|
and

\rho_{0}=\min_{n\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\lozenge}\left\{|\text{Im}\theta_{n}|\right\}>0.

(3.20)

For the poles
\zeta_{n}
with
\mathcal{N}\setminus\lozenge
, we want to trap them for jumps along small closed loops enclosing themselves,
respectively. And the jump matrix
V_{j}(z)
(
2.53
) also needs to be restricted for
j=1,...,6
. Recall the factorizations of the jump matrix:

For, j=1,4,

\displaystyle V_{j}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\bar{r}e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-re^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{-re^{it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{1}{1-|r|^{2}}&0&0\\
0&1-|r|^{2}&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\frac{\bar{r}e^{-it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right);

For, j=2,5,

\displaystyle V_{j}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&\bar{r}(\omega z)e^{-it\theta_{23}}&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&-r(\omega z)e^{it\theta_{23}}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&\frac{-r(\omega z)e^{it\theta_{23}}}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}}&0\\
0&0&1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&\frac{\bar{r}(\omega z)e^{-it\theta_{23}}}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}}&1\end{array}\right);

For, j=3,6,

\displaystyle V_{j}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z)e^{it\theta_{13}}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
-r(\omega^{2}z)e^{-it\theta_{13}}&0&1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
\frac{-r(\omega^{2}z)e^{-it\theta_{13}}}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}}&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&\frac{\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z)e^{it\theta_{13}}}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right).

We will utilize these factorizations to deform the jump contours so that the oscillating factor
e^{\pm it\theta_{12}}
are decaying in corresponding region, respectively.

Introduce the notations

\displaystyle I(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\emptyset,&\text{as }\xi>1,\\[4.0pt]
(-\infty,\xi_{8})\cup_{j=1}^{3}(\xi_{2j+1},\xi_{2j})\cup(\xi_{1},+\infty),&\text{as }-\frac{1}{8}<\xi<0,\\[4.0pt]
(\xi_{4},\xi_{3})\cup(\xi_{2},\xi_{1}),&\text{as }0\leq\xi<1,\\[4.0pt]
\mathbb{R},&\text{as }\xi<-\frac{1}{8},\\
\end{array}\right.

and
I^{\omega}(\xi)=\{\omega z:z\in I(\xi)\}
,
I^{\omega^{2}}(\xi)=\{\omega^{2}z:z\in I(\xi)\}
. Here we denote
the integration on
\emptyset
is zero.

Consider the following scalar RH problem

\blacktriangleright

\delta_{1}(z)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
;

\blacktriangleright

\delta_{1}(z)
has jump relation:

\displaystyle\delta_{1,+}(z)=\delta_{1,-}(z)(1-|r(z)|^{2}),\ z\in I(\xi),

\displaystyle\delta_{1,+}(z)=\delta_{1,-}(z),\ z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus I(\xi).

\blacktriangleright

\delta_{1}(z)\to 1
as
z\to\infty
.

By Plemelj formula, this RH problem admits solution

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\delta_{1}(z,\xi)=\exp\left(-i\int_{I(\xi)}\dfrac{\nu(s)ds}{s-z}\right),

(3.70)

where
\nu(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(1-|r(z)|^{2}).

We define

\displaystyle\Pi(z)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n\in\Delta_{1}}\dfrac{z-\zeta_{n}}{z-\bar{\zeta}_{n}},\ \Pi^{A}(z)=\prod_{m\in\Delta_{2}}\dfrac{z-\omega\zeta_{m}}{z-\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{m}};

(3.71)

\displaystyle H(z)

\displaystyle=H(z,\xi)=\Pi^{A}(z)\Pi(z)\delta_{1}(z,\xi)^{-1};

(3.72)

\displaystyle T_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=T_{1}(z,\xi)=\frac{H(\omega^{2}z)}{H(z)};

(3.73)

\displaystyle T_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=T_{2}(z,\xi)=T_{1}(\omega z)=\frac{H(z)}{H(\omega z)};

(3.74)

\displaystyle T_{3}(z)

\displaystyle=T_{3}(z,\xi)=T_{1}(\omega^{2}z)=\frac{H(\omega z)}{H(\omega^{2}z)};

(3.75)

\displaystyle T_{ij}(z)

\displaystyle=T_{ij}(z,\xi)=\frac{T_{i}(z)}{T_{j}(z)},\ i,j=1,2,3.

(3.76)

In the above formulas, we choose the principal branch of power and logarithm functions. Additionally, Introduce a positive constant
\tilde{\varrho}=\frac{1}{6}\underset{j\neq i\in\mathcal{N}}{\min}|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{j}|
and a set of characteristic functions
\mathcal{X}(z;\xi,j)
on the interval
\eta(\xi,j)\xi_{j}-\tilde{\varrho}<\eta(\xi,j)z<\eta(\xi,j)\xi_{j}
for
j=1,...,p(\xi)
, respectively. And
\eta(\xi,j)
is a constant depend on
\xi
and
j
:

\displaystyle\eta(\xi,j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(-1)^{j},&\text{as }-\frac{1}{8}<\xi<0;\\
(-1)^{j+1},&\text{as }0\leq\xi<1.\end{array}\right.

Proposition 3
.

The function defined by (
3.73
) and (
3.76
) has following properties

(a)

T_{1}
is meromorphic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, and for each
n\in\Delta_{1}
,
T_{1}(z)
has a simple pole at
\zeta_{n}
and a simple zero
at
\bar{\zeta}_{n}
, for each
m\in\Delta_{2}
,
T_{1}(z)
has a simple pole at
\omega\zeta_{m}
and a simple zero at
\omega\bar{\zeta}_{m}
;

(b)

\overline{T_{1}(\bar{z})}=T_{1}(\omega z)=T_{1}(z^{-1})
;

(c)

For
z\in I(\xi)
, as z approaching the real axis from above and below,
\delta_{1}(z)
has boundary values
\delta_{1,\pm}
, which satisfies:

\delta_{1,-}(z)=(1-|r(z)|^{2})\delta_{1,+}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in I(\xi),

(3.79)

which gives that

\displaystyle T_{1,-}(z)=(1-|r(z)|^{2})T_{1,+}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in I(\xi),

(3.80)

\displaystyle T_{1,+}(z)=(1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2})T_{1,-}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in I^{\omega}(\xi);

(3.81)

(d)

\lim_{z\to\infty}T_{1}(z)\triangleq T_{1}(\infty)
with
T_{1}(\infty)=1
;

(e)

T_{1}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}})
exists as a constants;

(f)

T_{1}(z)
is continuous at
z=0
, and

T_{1}(0)=T_{1}(\infty)=1;

(3.82)

(g)

As
z\to\xi_{j}
along any ray
\xi_{j}+e^{i\phi}\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
|\phi|<\pi
,

\displaystyle|T_{12}(z,\xi)-T_{12}^{(j)}(\xi)\left[\eta(\xi,j)(z-\xi_{j})\right]^{i\eta(\xi,j)\nu(\xi_{j})}|\lesssim\parallel r\parallel_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})}|z-\xi_{j}|^{1/2},

(3.83)

where
T_{12}^{(j)}(\xi)
is the complex unit

\displaystyle T_{12}^{(j)}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n\in\Delta_{1}}\left(\dfrac{\xi_{j}-\zeta_{n}}{\xi_{j}-\bar{\zeta}_{n}}\right)^{-2}\dfrac{\xi_{j}-\omega\zeta_{n}}{\xi_{j}-\omega\bar{\zeta}_{n}}\dfrac{\xi_{j}-\omega^{2}\zeta_{n}}{\xi_{j}-\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{n}}

\displaystyle\prod_{m\in\Delta_{2}}\left(\dfrac{\xi_{j}-\omega\zeta_{m}}{\xi_{j}-\omega^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{m}}\right)^{-2}\dfrac{\xi_{j}-\omega^{2}\zeta_{m}}{\xi_{j}-\bar{\zeta}_{m}}\dfrac{\xi_{j}-\zeta_{m}}{\xi_{j}-\omega\bar{\zeta}_{m}}e^{2i\beta(\xi_{j},\xi)},\ \ j=1,...,p(\xi).

In above function,

\displaystyle\beta_{j}(z,\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{I(\xi)}\frac{\nu(s)-\mathcal{X}(z;\xi,j)\nu(\xi_{j})}{s-z}ds-\eta(\xi,j)\nu(\xi_{j})\log\left(\eta(\xi,j)(z-\xi_{j}+\tilde{\varrho})\right).

Proof.

Properties (a), (b), (d) and (f) can be obtain by simple calculation from the definition of
T_{1}(z)
and
T_{ij}
in (
3.73
) and (
3.76
). (c) follows from the Plemelj formula. And for (g), analogously to
[
31
]
, rewrite

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(z,\xi)=\exp\left(i\beta_{j}(z,\xi)+\nu(\xi_{j})\eta(\xi,j)i\log\left(\eta(\xi,j)(z-\xi_{j})\right)\right),

(3.84)

and note the fact that

\displaystyle|(z-\xi_{j})^{\eta(\xi,j)i\nu(\xi_{j})}|\leq e^{-\pi\nu(\xi_{j})=\sqrt{1+|r(\xi_{j})|^{2}}},

(3.85)

and

|\beta_{j}(z,\xi)-\beta_{j}(\xi_{j},\xi)|\lesssim\parallel r\parallel_{H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R})}|z-\xi_{j}|^{1/2}.

Then, the result follows promptly.
For brevity, we omit computation.
∎

for

Additionally, introduce a positive constant

\displaystyle\varrho=\frac{1}{4}\min

RHP 2
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{(1)}(z)
which satisfies:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{(1)}(z)
is meromorphic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{(1)}
, where

is shown in Figure

;

\blacktriangleright

M^{(1)}(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{M^{(1)}(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M^{(1)}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M^{(1)}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}

and

;

\blacktriangleright

has continuous boundary values

on

and

0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\

0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in I(\xi);\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\

(3.95)

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{(1)}(z;y,t)=

Singularities: As

-\beta_{\pm}&-\alpha_{\pm}&-\alpha_{\pm}\end{array}\right)T(\pm\omega^{2})+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega^{2},

\displaystyle M^{(1)}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\alpha_{\pm}&-\beta_{\pm}&-\alpha_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\\
\alpha_{\pm}&\beta_{\pm}&\alpha_{\pm}\end{array}\right)T(\pm\omega)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega,

with

,

,

Residue conditions:
M^{(1)}
has simple poles at each point
\zeta_{n}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{n}
for

with

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M^{(1)}(z)=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}M^{(1)}(z)\left[T^{-1}(z)B_{n}T(z)\right].

(3.106)

Proof.

The triangular factors (
3.91
) trades poles
\zeta_{n}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{n}
to jumps on the disk boundaries

and

. Then by simple calculation we can obtain the residues condition and jump condition from (
1
), (
1
) (
2.53
), (
3.91
) and (

). The analyticity and symmetry of

. As for asymptotic behaviors, from

and (d) of Proposition

, we obtain the asymptotic behaviors of

.
∎

4
Hybrid
\bar{\partial}
-RH problem

In this section, by defining a new transformation for

M^{(1)}(z)
, we make continuous extension to the jump matrix
V^{(1)}(z)
to remove the jump contours
\mathbb{R}\cup\omega\mathbb{R}\cup\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}
,
so that we can take advantage of the decay/growth of
e^{2it\theta_{jk}(z)}
on new jump contours.

4.1
Opening
\bar{\partial}
-lenses in space-time regions
\xi<-{1}/{8}
and
\xi>1

From (
3.1
), there is no phase point in the space-time regions
\xi<-{1}/{8}
and
\xi>1
,
for which we can open the contours at
z=0
. For this purpose, we introduce some notations.

Fixed the angle
0<\varphi<\frac{\pi}{6}
sufficiently small, near the
jump contours
\mathbb{R}\cup\omega\mathbb{R}\cup\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}
,
we define domains

\displaystyle\Omega_{2n+1}^{k}=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ n\pi\leq\arg(\omega^{k}z)\leq n\pi+\varphi\right\},

(4.1)

\displaystyle\Omega_{2n+2}^{k}=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ (n+1)\pi-\varphi\leq\arg(\omega^{k}z)\leq(n+1)\pi\right\}.

(4.2)

where
n=0,1;\ k=0,1,2
. The boundaries of these domains are the following rays

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}^{k}=e^{(n-1)\pi i/2+i\varphi}R_{+},\hskip 14.22636ptn=1,3,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}^{k}=e^{n\pi i/2-i\varphi}R_{+},\hskip 14.22636ptn=2,4.

(4.4)

where
k=0,1,2
. In addition, for these cases, let

\displaystyle\Omega(\xi)=\underset{n=1,...,4}{\cup}\left(\Omega_{n}^{0}\cup\Omega_{n}^{1}\cup\Omega_{n}^{2}\right),

(4.5)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(2)}(\xi)=\underset{n\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\lozenge,k=0,..,5}{\cup}\partial\mathbb{D}_{n+kN_{0}},

(4.6)

\displaystyle{\Sigma}^{k}(\xi)=\Sigma_{1}^{k}\cup\Sigma_{2}^{k}\cup\Sigma_{3}^{k}\cup\Sigma_{4}^{k},\ \ k=0,1,2,

(4.7)

\displaystyle{\Sigma}(\xi)={\Sigma}^{0}(\xi)\cup{\Sigma}^{1}(\xi)\cup{\Sigma}^{2}(\xi),

(4.8)

which are shown in Figure
8
.

Figure 8:
For the case
\xi>1
, the signature table
\text{Im }\theta_{kj}(z)<0
in yellow domains
and
\text{Im }\theta_{kj}(z)>0
in blue domains; For the case
\xi<-1/8
, the signature table
\text{Im }\theta_{kj}(z)>0
in yellow domains
and
\text{Im }\theta_{kj}(z)<0
in blue domains. The circles around poles off critical lines
\text{Im }\theta_{kj}(z)=0
for
j,k=1,2,3
(here take
z_{n}
as an example).

Next we show that when angle
\varphi
is sufficiently small,
then
\text{Im }\theta_{jk}(z)>0
or
\text{Im }\theta_{jk}(z)<0
in opening domains (
4.1
)-(
4.2
) to
guarantee decay/growth of
e^{2it\theta_{jk}(z)}
on new jump contours.

Lemma 4
.

Let
f(x)=x+{1}/{x}
is a real-valued function for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
and each
\Omega_{i}
doesn’t intersect critical lines
\left\{z\in\mathbb{C};\text{Im }\theta_{12}(z)=0\right\}
and disks
\mathbb{D}_{n}
or
\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{n}
.

(i)
For
\xi>1
, fixed the angle
\varphi
sufficiently small and satisfy
1+{1}/{\xi}<2\cos 2\varphi<2
, then
there exists a positive constant
c(\xi)
such that

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\leq-c(\xi)|\sin\varphi|f(|z|),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},

(4.9)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\geq c(\xi)|\sin\varphi|f(|z|),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{3},\Omega_{4}.

(4.10)

(ii)
For
\xi<-{1}/{8}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\xi)
be the solution of equation
2\sqrt{x^{2}-5x+4}+x-1=1/|\xi|
,
let
\varphi
satisfy
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\xi)<2\cos 2\varphi+3<5
. Then there exists a positive constant
c(\xi)
such that

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\geq|\sin\varphi|f(|z|)c(\xi),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},

(4.11)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\leq-|\sin\varphi|f(|z|)c(\xi),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{3},\Omega_{4}.

(4.12)

Proof.

We prove the case (i) and take
z\in\Omega_{1}
as an example, and the other regions are similarly.
From (
3.5
), for
z=le^{i\phi}
, rewrite
\text{Im }\theta_{12}(z)
as

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)=\sqrt{3}f(l)\sin\phi\left(\xi+\frac{f(l)^{3}-(2\cos 2\phi+3)}{f(l)^{4}-(2\cos 2\phi+4)f(l)^{2}+(2\cos 2\phi+1)^{2}}\right).

Denote

\displaystyle h(x;a)=\frac{x-a}{x^{2}-x(a+1)+(a-2)^{2}},

(4.13)

with
x\geq 4
and
1<a\leq 5
. Then

\frac{f(l)^{3}-(2\cos 2\phi+3)}{f(l)^{4}-(2\cos 2\phi+4)f(l)^{2}+(2\cos 2\phi+1)^{2}}=h(f(l)^{2};2\cos 2\phi+3).

By simple calculation,
we have that

h(x;a)\in\left(\frac{1}{4-a},\frac{1}{2\sqrt{a^{2}-5a+4}+a-1}\right).

By using
1+{1}/{\xi}<2\cos 2\varphi<2
, we then have

\xi+h(f(l)^{2};2\cos 2\phi+3)\geq 0.

Similarly from assumption above, the estimate in the case
\xi<-{1}/{8}
also holds. Thus the result is obtained.
∎

Corollary 1
.

Let
z=le^{i\phi}=u+vi
, then
there exists a constant
c(\xi)>0
such that

For
\xi\in(-\infty,-\frac{1}{8})
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta(z)\geq c(\xi)v,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{3},\Omega_{4},

(4.14)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta(z)\leq-c(\xi)v,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}.

(4.15)

For
\xi\in(1,+\infty)
, it holds that

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta(z)\geq c(\xi)v,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},

(4.16)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta(z)\leq-c(\xi)v,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{3},\Omega_{4}.

(4.17)

The next key step is to make continuous extensions to the jump matrix
V^{(1)}(z)
off the
jump contours
\mathbb{R}\cup\omega\mathbb{R}\cup\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}

onto new contours along which the jump matrices are decaying. For this purpose, we
define the new matrix unknown functions
R^{(2)}(z,\xi)
as

For
\xi>1
,

R^{(2)}(z,\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&R_{j}(z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega_{j}^{0},j=1,2;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
R_{j}(z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega_{j}^{0},j=3,4;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&R_{j}(\omega z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{23}}&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{1}_{j},j=1,2;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&R_{j}(\omega z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{23}}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{1}_{j},j=3,4;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&R_{j}(\omega^{2}z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{13}}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{2}_{j},j=1,2;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
R_{j}(\omega^{2}z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{13}}&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{2}_{j},j=3,4;\\
\\
I,&elsewhere;\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.18)

for
\xi<-1/8
,

R^{(2)}(z,\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&R_{j}(z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega_{j}^{0},j=3,4;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
R_{j}(z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega_{j}^{0},j=1,2;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&R_{j}(\omega z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{23}}&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{1}_{j},j=3,4;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&R_{j}(\omega z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{23}}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{1}_{j},j=1,2;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&R_{j}(\omega^{2}z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{13}}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{2}_{j},j=3,4;\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
R_{j}(\omega^{2}z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{13}}&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{2}_{j},j=1,2;\\
\\
I,&elsewhere;\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.19)

where the functions
R_{j}
,
j=1,2,3,4
, is defined in following Proposition.

Proposition 4
.

R_{j}
:
\bar{\Omega}_{j}^{k}\to C
,
j=1,2,3,4;k=0,1,2
have boundary values as follows:
(i) For
\xi>1
,

\displaystyle R_{1}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{1}(z,\xi)T_{21}(z)&z\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
0&z\in\Sigma_{1},\\
\end{array},\hskip 5.69046ptR_{2}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&z\in\Sigma_{2},\\
p_{2}(z,\xi)T_{21}(z)&z\in\mathbb{R}^{-},\\
\end{array}

\displaystyle R_{3}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{3}(z,\xi)T_{12}(z)&z\in\mathbb{R}^{-},\\
0&z\in\Sigma_{3},\\
\end{array},\hskip 5.69046ptR_{4}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&z\in\Sigma_{4},\\
p_{4}(z,\xi)T_{12}(z)&z\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
\end{array}.

The functions
R_{j},j=1,2,3,4
have following property

\displaystyle|\bar{\partial}R_{j}(z)|\lesssim|p_{j}^{\prime}(sign(\text{Re}z)|z|)|+|z|^{-1/2}+|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\text{Re}z)|,\text{ for all $z\in\Omega_{j}^{k}$,}

(4.28)

\displaystyle R_{j}(z)=\bar{\partial}R_{j}(z)=0,\text{ for }z\in\Omega_{j}\text{ with }||z|-1|\leq\varepsilon,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}R_{j}(z)=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }z\in elsewhere.

(ii) For
\xi<-1/8
,

\displaystyle R_{1}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{1}(z,\xi)[T_{12}]_{+}(z)&z\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
0&z\in\Sigma_{1},\\
\end{array},\hskip 5.69046ptR_{2}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&z\in\Sigma_{2},\\
p_{2}(z,\xi)[T_{12}]_{+}(z)&z\in\mathbb{R}^{-},\\
\end{array}

\displaystyle R_{3}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{3}(z,\xi)[T_{21}]_{-}(z)&z\in\mathbb{R}^{-},\\
0&z\in\Sigma_{3},\\
\end{array},\hskip 5.69046ptR_{4}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&z\in\Sigma_{4},\\
p_{4}(z,\xi)[T_{21}(z)]_{-}&z\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
\end{array}.

The functions
R_{j},j=1,2,3,4
have following property

\displaystyle|\bar{\partial}R_{j}(z)|\lesssim|p_{j}^{\prime}(sign(\text{Re}z)|z|)|+|z|^{-1}+|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\text{Re}z)|,\text{ for all $z\in\Omega_{j}^{k}$.}

(4.37)

\displaystyle R_{j}(z)=\bar{\partial}R_{j}(z)=0,\text{ for }z\in\Omega_{j}\text{ with }||z|-1|\leq\varepsilon,

(4.38)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}R_{j}(z)=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }z\in elsewhere.

Proof.

For brief, we
take
R_{1}(z)
as an example. The extension of
R_{1}(z)
can be constructed by:

\displaystyle R_{1}(z)=(1-\mathcal{X}(\text{Re}z))R_{11}(z),

(4.39)

where

\displaystyle R_{11}(z)=

\displaystyle p_{1}(|z|)T_{12}(z)\cos(k_{0}\arg z),\hskip 14.22636ptk_{0}=\frac{\pi}{2\varphi}.

(4.40)

Obviously, this construction make
R_{1}
admits (
4.29
).
The other cases of
R_{j}
are easily inferred. Denote
z=le^{i\phi}
, then we have
\bar{\partial}=\frac{e^{i\phi}}{2}\left(\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\phi}\right)
. therefore,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\left((1-\mathcal{X}(l\cos\phi))R_{11}(z)\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{i\phi}}{2}T_{12}(z)p_{1}^{\prime}(l)(1-\mathcal{X}(l\cos\phi))\cos(k_{0}\phi)

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{i\phi}ik_{0}}{2l}T_{12}(z)p_{1}(l)\sin(k_{0}\phi)

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{i\phi}}{2}T_{12}(z)p_{1}(l)\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(l\cos\phi)\left(\sin\phi-\cos(k_{0}\phi)\right).

(4.41)

There are two ways to bound last term. First we use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and obtain

|p_{1}(l)|=|p_{1}(l)-p_{1}(0)|=|\int_{0}^{l}p_{1}^{\prime}(s)ds|\leq\parallel p_{1}^{\prime}(s)\parallel_{L^{2}}l^{1/2}\lesssim l^{1/2}.

(4.42)

And note that
T(z)
is a bounded function in
\bar{\Omega}_{1}
. Then the boundedness of (
4.28
) follows immediately. On the other side,
p_{1}(l)\in L^{\infty}
, which implies (
4.37
).
∎

4.2
Opening
\bar{\partial}
-lenses in space-time region
-{1}/{8}<\xi<1

From (
3.1
), there are four and eight phase points
in the space-time regions
0<\xi<1
and
-{1}/{8}<\xi<0
respectively,
for which we should open three jump contours
\omega^{n}\mathbb{R},n=0,1,2
at phase points
\xi_{j}\in\mathbb{R},j=2,\cdots,p(\xi)-1
.
In the follow, to simplify notations, we set

A^{\omega^{0}}=A,\ \ A^{\omega^{1}}=A^{\omega},
for a symbol
A
.

\blacktriangleright
At phase points
\xi_{j}\in\omega^{n}\mathbb{R},j=2,\cdots,p(\xi)-1
, we define rays

For
k=1,3
,

\rho\in(0,\frac{|\xi_{j+(-1)^{j+(k+1)/2}}-\xi_{j}|}{4\cos\varphi})
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{jk}^{\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\xi_{j}+e^{i[(k/2+1/2+j)\pi+(-1)^{j+1}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0>\xi>-1/8\\
\xi_{j}+e^{i[(k/2-1/2+j)\pi+(-1)^{j}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0\leq\xi<1.\end{array}\right.

For
k=2,4,

\rho\in(0,\frac{|\xi_{j+(-1)^{j+(k+1)/2}}-\xi_{j}|}{4\cos\varphi})
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{jk}^{\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\xi_{j}+e^{i[(k/2-1+j)\pi+(-1)^{j+1}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0>\xi>-1/8,\\
\xi_{j}+e^{i[(k/2+j)\pi+(-1)^{j}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0\leq\xi<1,\end{array}\right.

\blacktriangleright
At the phase points
\xi_{j}\in\mathbb{R},j=1,p(\xi)
, we define rays

\displaystyle\Sigma_{j1}^{\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\omega^{n}\xi_{j}+e^{(1+j)\pi i+(-1)^{j+1}i\varphi}\mathbb{R}^{+},&\ 0>\xi>-1/8\\
\omega^{n}\xi_{j}+e^{j\pi i+(-1)^{j}i\varphi}\rho,&\ 0\leq\xi<1\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{j2}^{\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\xi_{j}+e^{(1+j)\pi i+(-1)^{j}i\varphi}\mathbb{R}^{+},&\ 0>\xi>-1/8\\
\xi_{j}+e^{j\pi i+(-1)^{j+1}i\varphi}\rho,&\ 0\leq\xi<1\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{j3}^{\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\xi_{j}+e^{j\pi i+(-1)^{j+1}i\varphi}\rho,&\ 0>\xi>-1/8\\
\xi_{j}+e^{(1+j)\pi i+(-1)^{j}i\varphi}\mathbb{R}^{+},&\ 0\leq\xi<1\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{j4}^{\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\xi_{j}+e^{j\pi i+(-1)^{j}i\varphi}\rho,&\ 0>\xi>-1/8\\
\xi_{j}+e^{(1+j)\pi i+(-1)^{j+1}i\varphi}\mathbb{R}^{+},&\ 0\leq\xi<1.\end{array}\right.

\blacktriangleright
Denote
\xi_{(j+1)/2}=(\xi_{j}+\xi_{j+1})/2
, we define rays

For k=1,3,
\rho\in(0,\frac{|\xi_{j+(-1)^{j+(k+1)/2}}-\xi_{j}|}{4\cos\varphi})
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{jk}^{{}^{\prime}\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\xi_{(j+1)/2}+e^{i[(k/2-1/2+j)\pi+(-1)^{j}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0>\xi>-1/8,\\
\xi_{(j+1)/2}+e^{i[(k/2+1/2+j)\pi+(-1)^{j+1}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0\leq\xi<1,\end{array}\right.

For
k=2,4,

\rho\in(0,\frac{|\xi_{j+(-1)^{j+(k+1)/2}}-\xi_{j}|}{4\cos\varphi})
.

\displaystyle\Sigma_{jk}^{{}^{\prime}\omega^{n}}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\xi_{(j+1)/2}+e^{i[(k/2-1+j)\pi+(-1)^{j+1}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0>\xi>-1/8,\\
\xi_{(j+1)/2}+e^{i[(k/2+j)\pi+(-1)^{j}\varphi]}\rho,&\ 0\leq\xi<1.\end{array}\right.\hskip 5.69046pt

These contours separate complex plane
\mathbb{C}
into sectors denoted by
\Omega_{jk}^{\omega^{n}},j=1,\cdots,p(\xi);

k=1,\cdots,4
, which
are shown in Figure
9
.
We fix a sufficiently small angle
0<\varphi<\frac{\pi}{6}
such that
each
\Omega_{jk}^{\omega^{n}}
doesn’t intersect with critical lines
{\rm Im}\theta_{jk}(z)=0
as well as
disks
\mathbb{D}_{n}
or
\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{n}
.

By using these rays defined above, we then define new contours obtained when opening jump contours

\omega^{n}\mathbb{R},n=0,1,2
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}^{\omega^{n}}(\xi)=(\underset{j=1,..,p(\xi)}{\underset{k=1,...,4,}{\cup}}\Sigma_{jk}^{\omega^{n}})\cup(\underset{j=1,..,p(\xi)}{\underset{k=1,...,4,}{\cup}}\Sigma_{jk}^{{}^{\prime}\omega^{n}}),\ \ n=0,1,2\hskip 14.22636pt

(4.59)

\displaystyle\ddot{\Sigma}(\xi)=\tilde{\Sigma}(\xi)\cup\tilde{\Sigma}^{\omega}(\xi)\cup\tilde{\Sigma}^{\omega^{2}}(\xi),

(4.60)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(2)}(\xi)=\ddot{\Sigma}(\xi)\underset{n\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\lozenge}{\cup}\left(\partial\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{n}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}\right).

(4.61)

Further we also define opened domains along jump contours
\omega^{n}\mathbb{R},n=0,1,2
:

\displaystyle\Omega(\xi)=\underset{j=1,..,p(\xi)}{\underset{k=1,...,4,}{\cup}}\left(\Omega_{jk}\cup\Omega_{jk}^{\omega}\cup\Omega_{jk}^{\omega^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\Omega^{(m)}(\xi)=S_{m}\setminus\Omega,\ m=1,...,6.
