Figures (a) and (b) are corresponding to the
0\leq\xi<1
and
-{1}/{8}<\xi<0
, respectively.
As illustrative example, here we only give the graph as Re
z>0
.
\Sigma_{ij}
separate
complex plane
\mathbb{C}
into some sectors denoted by
\Omega_{ij}
. The yellow domains are produced by the first factorizations of jump matrices;
The yellow domains are produced by the second factorizations of jump matrices

Lemma 5
.

There exists a constant
c(\xi)>0
relied on
\xi=\in(-1/8,1)
and a positive real-value function
g(x;\xi)
with
\lim_{x\to\infty}g(x;\xi)=0
and
\lim_{x\to\infty}(\text{Re}^{2}z-\xi_{i}^{2})g(x;\xi)=g_{0}\in(0,1)
. Then the imaginary part of phase function (
3.5
)
{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)
have following estimation for
i=1,...,p(\xi)
:

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\geq c(\xi){\rm Im}z({\rm Re}^{2}z-\xi_{i}^{2})g({\rm Re}z;\xi),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{i2},\Omega_{i4};

(4.62)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\leq-c(\xi){\rm Im}z({\rm Re}^{2}z-\xi_{i}^{2})g({\rm Re}z;\xi),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{i1},\Omega_{i3}.

(4.63)

Proof.

We only give the detail of Case III and take
z\in\Omega_{14}
as an example, and the other regions are similarly.
Denote
z=x+yi
with
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and

\displaystyle k=-\frac{1}{4}\left(z-\frac{1}{z}\right),\hskip 14.22636ptk_{1}=-\frac{1}{4}\left(\xi_{1}-\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right).

(4.64)

Take notice that
\xi_{1}>1
, so
0<k_{1}<1
.
And denote

\displaystyle u\triangleq\text{Re}(k)=x\left(1-\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}\right),

(4.65)

\displaystyle v\triangleq\text{Im}(k)=y\left(1+\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}\right),

(4.66)

with
0<v\leq(x-\xi_{1})\tan\varphi\left(1+\frac{1}{x^{2}+\tan^{2}\varphi(x-\xi_{1})^{2}}\right)
and
u>1
.
Then the imaginary part of phase function (
3.5
)
{\rm Im}\theta(z)
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)=\sqrt{3}v\left[\xi+\frac{u^{2}+v^{2}-1}{u^{4}+v^{4}+2u^{2}+1-2v^{2}+2u^{2}v^{2}}\right].

(4.67)

Denote

\displaystyle f_{0}(u,v)\triangleq\frac{u^{2}+v^{2}-1}{u^{4}+v^{4}+2u^{2}+1-2v^{2}+2u^{2}v^{2}}>0.

(4.68)

Our first goal is to show
{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\geq c(\xi+f(u,0))
, with a positive constant
c
. Note that
u^{2}+v^{2}>1
,

\displaystyle f_{0}(u,v)=\frac{1}{u^{2}+v^{2}-1+\frac{4u^{2}}{u^{2}+v^{2}-1}}.

(4.69)

therefore,
f_{0}(u,v)
has bounded nonzero maximum and minimum value, then there exists a positive constant
c^{\prime}
making that
f_{0}(u,v)\geq c^{\prime}f_{0}(u,0)
. Next, we rewrite

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{1-k_{1}^{2}}{(k_{1}^{2}+1)^{2}},

then together with
v=0\Leftrightarrow y=0
,

\displaystyle\xi+f(u,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}-1}{(u^{2}+1)^{2}}-\frac{k_{1}^{2}-1}{(k_{1}^{2}+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{u^{2}-k_{1}^{2}}{(k_{1}^{2}+1)^{2}(u^{2}+1)^{2}}\left(3+k_{1}^{2}+(1-k_{1}^{2})u^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=(x^{2}-\xi_{1}^{2})(1-\dfrac{1}{x^{2}\xi_{1}^{2}})\dfrac{1+3k_{1}^{2}+(1-k_{1}^{2})(x^{2}+x^{-2})}{(k_{1}^{2}+1)^{2}(x^{2}+x^{-2}-1)^{2}}.

Let
g(x;\xi)=(1-\dfrac{1}{x^{2}\xi_{1}^{2}})\dfrac{1+3k_{1}^{2}+(1-k_{1}^{2})(x^{2}+x^{-2})}{(k_{1}^{2}+1)^{2}(x^{2}+x^{-2}-1)^{2}}>0
. Then the proof is completed.
∎

Corollary 2
.

There exist three positive constants
c_{1}(\xi)
,
c_{2}(\xi)
and a large
R(\xi)>>\xi_{1}
relied on
\xi\in(-1/8,1)
, such that the imaginary part of phase function (
3.5
)
{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)
have following estimation for
i=1,...,p(\xi)
:

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\geq c_{1}(\xi){\rm Im}z({\rm Re}z-\xi_{i}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\Omega_{i2},\Omega_{i4},\ |{\rm Re}z|<R(\xi);

(4.70)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\leq-c_{1}(\xi){\rm Im}z({\rm Re}z-\xi_{i}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\Omega_{i1},\Omega_{i3},\ |{\rm Re}z|<R(\xi);

(4.71)

and

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\geq c_{2}(\xi){\rm Im}z,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{i2},\Omega_{i4},\ |{\rm Re}z|>R(\xi);

(4.72)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\theta_{12}(z)\leq-c_{2}(\xi){\rm Im}z,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as }z\in\Omega_{i1},\Omega_{i3},\ |{\rm Re}z|>R(\xi).

(4.73)

Introduce following functions for brief:

for
\xi>1

\displaystyle p_{1}(z,\xi)=p_{2}(z,\xi)=r(z),\ p_{3}(z,\xi)=p_{4}(z,\xi)=\bar{r}(z).

(4.74)

for
\xi<-1/8

\displaystyle p_{1}(z,\xi)=p_{2}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-\bar{r}(z)}{1-|r(z)|^{2}},\ p_{3}(z,\xi)=p_{4}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-r(z)}{1-|r(z)|^{2}}.

(4.75)

As in Case II and Case III, for
j=1,...,p(\xi)
,

\displaystyle p_{j1}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-\bar{r}(z)}{1-|r(z)|^{2}},\hskip 14.22636ptp_{j3}(z,\xi)=\bar{r}(z),

(4.76)

\displaystyle p_{j2}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-r(z)}{1-|r(z)|^{2}},\hskip 17.07182ptp_{j4}(z,\xi)=r(z).

(4.77)

Besides, from
r\in\mathbb{R}(\mathbb{R})
, it also has
p_{1}^{\prime}(z)
and
p_{2}^{\prime}(z)
exist and are in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cup L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. And their
L^{2}
-norm and
L^{\infty}
-norm can be controlled by the norm of
r(z)
.
By symmetry, denote

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=p_{2}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=r(\omega^{2}z),\ p_{3}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=p_{4}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z),

(4.78)

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=p_{2}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=r(\omega z),\ p_{3}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=p_{4}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=\bar{r}(\omega z),

(4.79)

for
\xi>1
. And

\displaystyle p_{j1}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z)}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}},\hskip 14.22636ptp_{j3}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z),

(4.80)

\displaystyle p_{j2}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-r(\omega^{2}z)}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}},\hskip 17.07182ptp_{j4}^{\omega}(z,\xi)=r(\omega^{2}z),

(4.81)

\displaystyle p_{j1}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-\bar{r}(\omega z)}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}},\hskip 14.22636ptp_{j3}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=\bar{r}(\omega z),

(4.82)

\displaystyle p_{j2}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=\dfrac{-r(\omega z)}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}},\hskip 17.07182ptp_{j4}^{\omega^{2}}(z,\xi)=r(\omega z),

(4.83)

for
1>\xi>-\frac{1}{8}
and
j=1,...,p(\xi)
.

The next step is to construct a matrix function
R^{(2)}
.
Usually, we need to remove jump on
\mathbb{R}
,
\omega\mathbb{R}
and
\omega^{2}\mathbb{R}
, and have some mild control on
\bar{\partial}R^{(2)}
sufficiently to ensure that the
\bar{\partial}
-contribution to the long-time asymptotics of
q(x,t)
is negligible like in
[
31
]
. But we have extra singularity on the boundary.
Hence, to deal with the singularity at
\varkappa_{k}
,
k=1,...,6
, we introduce a fixed cutoff function
\mathcal{X}(z)
in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R},[0,1])
with support near
1
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}0,&|z-1|>2\varepsilon,\\[4.0pt]
1,&|z-1|<\varepsilon,\end{array}\right.

where
\varepsilon
is a small enough positive constant satisfying
the support of
\mathcal{X}(z)
doesn’t contain any of phase points with

\varepsilon<\frac{1}{8}\underset{1\leq j\neq k\leq p(\xi)}{\min}|\xi_{k}-\xi_{j}|,

and the support of
\mathcal{X}(\text{Im}z+1)\mathcal{X}(\text{Re}z)
is disjoint with
\ddot{\Sigma}(\xi)
.
Such constant
\varepsilon
indeed exists.

In fact, at the case
\xi\in(-\infty,-1/8)\cup(1,+\infty)
without no phase point, so these requirements are easy to achieve. At the case
\xi\in(-1/8,0)
, there are two
phase points
\xi_{2}
,
\xi_{3}=1/\xi_{2}
near
1
. So
1\neq\frac{\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}{2}
. Take
\varepsilon
small enough, then it achieve above requirements. And the case
\xi\in[0,1)
is same as
\xi\in(-1/8,0)
.

We now define continuous extension functions for the Case II and Case III,

R^{(2)}(z,\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&R_{kj}(z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega_{kj},j=2,4,\ k=1,...,p(\xi);\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
R_{kj}(z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega_{kj},j=1,3\ k=1,...,p(\xi);\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&R_{kj}(\omega^{2}z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{13}}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{\omega}_{j},j=2,4\ k=1,...,p(\xi);\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
R_{kj}(\omega^{2}z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{13}}&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{\omega}_{j},j=1,3\ k=1,...,p(\xi);\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&R_{kj}(\omega z,\xi)e^{-it\theta_{23}}&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{\omega^{2}}_{j},j=2,4\ k=1,...,p(\xi);\\
\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&R_{kj}(\omega z,\xi)e^{it\theta_{23}}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Omega^{\omega^{2}}_{j},j=1,3\ k=1,...,p(\xi);\\
\\
I,&elsewhere;\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.86)

where the functions
R_{kj}
,
k=1,...,p(\xi)
,
j=1,2,3,4
are given by following Proposition.

Proposition 5
.

As in Case II and Case III, the functions
R_{kj}
:
\bar{\Omega}_{kj}\to\mathbb{C}
,
k=1,...,p(\xi)
,
j=1,2,3,4
have boundary values as follows:

\displaystyle R_{k1}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{k1}(\xi_{k},\xi)T_{12}^{(k)}(\xi)\left(\eta(z-\xi_{k})\right)^{2i\eta\nu(\xi_{k})}&z\in\Sigma_{k1},\\
p_{k1}(z,\xi)[T_{12}]_{+}(z)&z\in I_{k1},\\
\end{array}

\displaystyle R_{k2}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{k2}(z,\xi)[T_{12}]_{-}(z)^{-1}&z\in I_{k2},\\
p_{k2}(\xi_{k},\xi)T_{12}^{(k)}(\xi)^{-1}\left(\eta(z-\xi_{k})\right)^{-2i\eta\nu(\xi_{k})}&z\in\Sigma_{k2},\\
\end{array}

\displaystyle R_{k3}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{k3}(\xi_{k},\xi)T_{12}^{(k)}(\xi)\left(\eta(z-\xi_{k})\right)^{2i\eta\nu(\xi_{k})}&z\in\Sigma_{k3},\\
p_{k3}(z,\xi)T_{12}(z)&z\in I_{k3},\\
\end{array}

\displaystyle R_{k4}(z,\xi)=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p_{k4}(z,\xi)T_{12}(z)^{-1}&z\in I_{k4},\\
p_{k4}(\xi_{k},\xi)T_{12}^{(k)}(\xi)^{-1}\left(\eta(z-\xi_{k})\right)^{-2i\eta\nu(\xi_{k})}&z\in\Sigma_{k4},\end{array}

where
I_{kj}
is specified in (
3.1
)-(
3.1
),
\eta=\eta(\xi,k)
defined in (
3.2
). And
R_{kj}
has following property:

\displaystyle|R_{kj}(z,\xi)|\lesssim\sin^{2}(k_{0}\arg(z-\xi_{k}))+\left(1+\text{Re}(z)^{2}\right)^{-1/2},\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{kj}$},

(4.95)

\displaystyle|\bar{\partial}R_{kj}(z,\xi)|\lesssim|p_{kj}^{\prime}(\text{Re}z)|+|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\text{Re}z)|+|z-\xi_{k}|^{-1/2},\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{kj}$.}

(4.96)

\displaystyle|\bar{\partial}R_{kj}(z,\xi)|\lesssim|p_{kj}^{\prime}(\text{Re}z)|+|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\text{Re}z)|+|z-\xi_{k}|^{-1},\text{for all $z\in\Omega_{kj}$.}

(4.97)

And

\displaystyle R_{kj}(z)=\bar{\partial}R_{kj}(z)=0,\text{ for all }z\in\Omega_{kj}\text{ with }|\text{Re}z\pm 1|<\varepsilon,

(4.98)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}R_{kj}(z,\xi)=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if }z\in elsewhere.

(4.99)

Proof.

For the region
\omega_{kj}
containing
\pm 1
, we construct the function
R_{kj}
by
R_{kj}(z)=(1-\mathcal{X}(\text{Re}z))\tilde{R}_{kj}(z)
as same as the case in Proposition
4
. Through multiply by the cutoff function
(1-\mathcal{X}(\text{Re}z))
, we achieve (
4.98
). And for the regions away from 1,
we give the details for
R_{14}
when
-\frac{1}{8}<\xi<0
only. The other cases are easily inferred. Note that
1\notin\Omega_{14}
, so
1-\mathcal{X}(\text{Re}z)\equiv 1
in
\Omega_{14}
. Using the constants
T_{12}^{(k)}(\xi)
defined in proposition
3
, we give the extension of
R_{14}(z,\xi)
on
\Omega_{14}
:

\displaystyle R_{14}(z,\xi)=

\displaystyle p_{14}(\xi_{1},\xi)T_{12}^{(1)}(\xi)^{-2}(z-\xi_{1})^{-2i\nu(\xi_{1})}\left[1-\cos\big(k_{0}\arg(z-\xi_{1})\big)\right]

\displaystyle+\cos\big(k_{0}\arg(z-\xi_{1})\big)p_{14}(\text{Re}z,\xi)T(z)^{-2}.

(4.100)

Let
z-\xi_{1}=le^{i\psi}=u+vi
,
l,\psi,u,v\in\mathbb{R}
. And from
r\in H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})
, which means
p_{14}\in H^{2,1}(R)
we have
|p_{14}(u)|\lesssim(1+u^{2})^{-1/2}
. Together with (
3.85
) we have (
4.95
). Since

\bar{\partial}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{u}+i\partial_{v}\right)=\frac{e^{i\psi}}{2}\left(\partial_{l}+il^{-1}\partial_{\psi}\right),

we have

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}R_{14}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(p_{14}(u,\xi)T(z)^{-2}-p_{14}(\xi_{1},\xi)T_{1}(\xi)^{-2}(z-\xi_{1})^{-2i\nu(\xi_{1})}\right)\bar{\partial}\cos(k_{0}\psi)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}T(z)^{-2}p_{14}^{\prime}(u,\xi)\cos(k_{0}\psi).

(4.101)

Substitute (
3.83
) into above equation, and using

\displaystyle|p_{14}(u,\xi)-p_{14}(\xi_{1},\xi)|=|\int_{\xi_{1}}^{u}p_{14}^{\prime}(s,\xi)ds|\leq\parallel p_{14}^{\prime}\parallel_{2}|u-\xi|^{1/2},

(4.102)

(
4.96
) comes immediately. If we simply use the boundedness of
p_{14}
, we obtain (
4.97
).
∎

In addition,
R^{(2)}
achieves the symmetry:

R^{(2)}(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{R^{(2)}(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{R^{(2)}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{R^{(2)}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}=\overline{R^{(2)}(\bar{z}^{-1})}.

(4.103)

4.3
A hybrid
\bar{\partial}
-RH problem and its decomposition

We now use
R^{(2)}
to define the new transformation

M^{(2)}(z;y,t)\triangleq M^{(2)}(z)=M^{(1)}(z)R^{(2)}(z),

(4.104)

which satisfies the following mixed
\bar{\partial}
-RH problem.

RHP 3
.

Find a matrix valued function
M^{(2)}(z)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{(2)}(z)
is continuous and has sectionally continuous first partial derivatives in

\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\Sigma^{(2)}(\xi)\cup\left\{\zeta_{n},\bar{\zeta}_{n}\right\}_{n\in\lozenge}\right)
, and is meromorphic out
\bar{\Omega}
;

\blacktriangleright
Symmetry:
M^{(2)}(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{M^{(2)}(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M^{(2)}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M^{(2)}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}
and
M^{(2)}(z)=\overline{M^{(2)}(\bar{z}^{-1})}
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
M^{(2)}
has continuous boundary values
M^{(2)}_{\pm}
on
\Sigma^{(2)}(\xi)
and

M^{(2)}_{+}(z)=M^{(2)}_{-}(z)V^{(2)}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\Sigma^{(2)}(\xi),

(4.105)

where for
\xi\in(1,+\infty)

V^{(2)}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T^{-1}(z)G(z)T(z),&z\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}\cap(\underset{k=1}{\cup^{3}}S_{2k});\\[12.0pt]
T^{-1}(z)G^{-1}(z)T(z),&z\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}\cap(\underset{k=1}{\cup^{3}}S_{2k-1});\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{rT_{21}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\frac{\bar{r}T_{12}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{-it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\mathbb{R};\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&\frac{-r(\omega z)T_{32}e^{it\theta_{23}}\mathcal{X}(|z|)}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&\frac{\bar{r}(\omega z)e^{-it\theta_{23}}T_{23}\mathcal{X}(|z|)}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}}&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\omega^{2}\mathbb{R};\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
\frac{-r(\omega^{2}z)T_{13}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{-it\theta_{13}}}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}}&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&\frac{\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z)T_{31}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{it\theta_{13}}}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\omega\mathbb{R};\\
\end{array}\right.,

(4.106)

and for
\xi\in(-1/8,1)

V^{(2)}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\underset{z^{\prime}\in\Omega\to z\in\ddot{\Sigma}(\xi)}{\lim}R^{(2)}(z^{\prime})&z\in\ddot{\Sigma}(\xi)\cap(\underset{m=1}{\cup^{3}}S_{2m-1});\\[12.0pt]
\underset{z^{\prime}\in\Omega\to z\in\ddot{\Sigma}(\xi)}{\lim}R^{(2)}(z^{\prime})^{-1}&z\in\ddot{\Sigma}(\xi)\cap(\underset{m=1}{\cup^{3}}S_{2m});\\[12.0pt]
T^{-1}(z)G(z)T(z),&z\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}\cap(\underset{m=1}{\cup^{3}}S_{2m});\\[12.0pt]
T^{-1}(z)G^{-1}(z)T(z),&z\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}\cap(\underset{m=1}{\cup^{3}}S_{2m-1});\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{rT_{21}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\frac{\bar{r}T_{12}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{-it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\mathbb{R};\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&\frac{-r(\omega z)T_{32}e^{it\theta_{23}}\mathcal{X}(|z|)}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&\frac{\bar{r}(\omega z)e^{-it\theta_{23}}T_{23}\mathcal{X}(|z|)}{1-|r(\omega z)|^{2}}&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\omega^{2}\mathbb{R};\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
\frac{-r(\omega^{2}z)T_{13}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{-it\theta_{13}}}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}}&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&\frac{\bar{r}(\omega^{2}z)T_{31}\mathcal{X}(|z|)e^{it\theta_{13}}}{1-|r(\omega^{2}z)|^{2}}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\omega\mathbb{R};\\
\end{array}\right.;

(4.107)

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(z)=

\displaystyle I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty;

(4.108)

\blacktriangleright

\bar{\partial}
-Derivative: For
z\in\mathbb{C}

we have

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}M^{(2)}=M^{(2)}\bar{\partial}R^{(2)};

(4.109)

\blacktriangleright
Singularities: The limiting values of
M^{(2)}(z)
as
z
approaches one of the points
\varkappa_{l}=e^{\frac{i\pi(l-1)}{3}}
,
l=1,...,6
have pole singularities with leading terms of a specific
matrix structure

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp 1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm 1,

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega^{2},

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\\
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega,

with
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}=\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\alpha}^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}=\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\beta}^{(2)}_{\pm}
and
M^{(2)}(z)^{-1}
has same specific
matrix structure with
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}
replaced by
\tilde{\alpha}^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\tilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{\pm}
;

\blacktriangleright
Residue conditions:
M^{(2)}
has simple poles at each point
\zeta_{n}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{n}
for
n\in\lozenge
with:

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M^{(2)}(z)=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}M^{(2)}(z)\left[T^{-1}(z)B_{n}T(z)\right].

(4.119)

Unlike the classical case in
[
31
]
, our construction of
R^{(2)}
will not completely remove the jump of
M^{(1)}
. As shown above,
M^{(2)}
still has jump near the singularity
\varkappa_{k}
on
l_{k}
,
k=1,...,6
. As reward for it, near singularity
\varkappa_{k}
,
R^{(2)}\equiv I
with
\bar{\partial}R^{(2)}\equiv 0
.

To solve RHP
3
, we decompose it into a model RH problem for
M^{R}(z;y,t)\triangleq M^{R}(z)
with
\bar{\partial}R^{(2)}\equiv 0
and a pure
\bar{\partial}
-Problem with nonzero
\bar{\partial}
-derivatives.
First we establish a RH problem for the
M^{R}(z)
as follows.

RHP 4
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{R}(z)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{R}(z)
is meromorphic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{(2)}
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
M^{R}
has continuous boundary values
M^{R}_{\pm}
on
\Sigma^{(2)}
and

M^{R}_{+}(z)=M^{R}_{-}(z)V^{(2)}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\Sigma^{(2)};

(4.120)

\blacktriangleright
Symmetry:
M^{R}(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{M^{R}(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M^{R}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M^{R}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}

and
M^{R}(z)=\overline{M^{R}(\bar{z}^{-1})}
;

\blacktriangleright

\bar{\partial}
-Derivative:
\bar{\partial}R^{(2)}=0
, for
z\in\mathbb{C}
;

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{R}(z)=

\displaystyle I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty;

(4.121)

\blacktriangleright
Singularities: As
z\rightarrow\varkappa_{l}=e^{\frac{i\pi(l-1)}{3}},l=1,...,6
, the limit of
M^{R}(z)
have

pole singularities

\displaystyle M^{R}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp 1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm 1,

\displaystyle M^{R}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega^{2},

\displaystyle M^{R}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\\
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega,

with
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}=\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\alpha}^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}=\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\beta}^{(2)}_{\pm}
and
M^{(2)}(z)^{-1}
has same specific
matrix structure with
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}
replaced by
\tilde{\alpha}^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\tilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{\pm}
;

\blacktriangleright
Residue conditions:
M^{R}
has simple poles at each point
\zeta_{n}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{n}
for
n\in\lozenge
with:

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M^{R}(z)=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}M^{R}(z)\left[T^{-1}(z)B_{n}T(z)\right].

(4.131)

Compared with the case
\xi\in(1,+\infty)
, the jump matrix
V^{(2)}
in the case
\xi\in(-1/8,1)

has additional portion on
\Sigma_{jk}
and
\Sigma_{jk}^{\prime}
. So this case is more difficult to deal with. Denote
\mathbb{B}_{j}
as the neighborhood of
\varkappa_{j}
,
j=1,...,6
with

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{j}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\ ;\text{ |Re}\left({z}/{\varkappa_{j}}\right)-1|<2\varepsilon,\ |\text{Im}\left({z}/{\varkappa_{j}}\right)|<2\varepsilon\}.

(4.132)

Denote
U(\xi)
as the union set of neighborhood of
\xi_{j}
,
\omega\xi_{j}
and
\omega^{2}\xi_{j}
,

\displaystyle U(\xi)=\underset{j=1,...,p(\xi)}{\cup}\left(U_{\xi_{j}}\cup U_{\omega\xi_{j}}\cup U_{\omega^{2}\xi_{j}}\right),\ U_{\xi_{j}}=\left\{z;|z-\xi_{j}|\leq\varrho^{0}\right\},

\displaystyle U_{\omega\xi_{j}}=\left\{z;|z-\omega\xi_{j}|\leq\varrho^{0}\right\},\ U_{\omega^{2}\xi_{j}}=\left\{z;|z-\omega^{2}\xi_{j}|\leq\varrho^{0}\right\},\ j=1,...,p(\xi).

Here,
\varrho^{0}
is a small enough positive constant make that
U(\xi)
and
\mathbb{B}_{j}
are disjoint, and
\varrho^{0}\leq\min\left\{\varrho,\ \frac{1}{8}\underset{j\neq i\in\mathcal{N}}{\min}|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|,\ \frac{1}{8}\underset{j\in\mathcal{N}}{\min}|\xi_{j}\pm 1|\right\}
. Then this additional part of jump matrix
V^{(2)}
has following estimation.

Proposition 6
.

For
1\leq q\leq+\infty
, there exists a positive constant
K_{q}
such that the jump matrix
V^{(2)}
defined in (
4.107
)
has the estimate

\displaystyle\parallel V^{(2)}-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{kj}\setminus U(\xi_{k}))}=\mathcal{O}(e^{-K_{q}t}),\ t\to\infty,

(4.133)

where
k=1,...,p(\xi)
and
j=1,...,4
.
Also for
1\leq q<+\infty
, there also exists a positive constant
K_{q}^{\prime}
such that the jump matrix
V^{(2)}
has the estimate

\displaystyle\parallel V^{(2)}-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{kj}^{\prime})}=\mathcal{O}(e^{-K_{q}^{\prime}t}),

(4.134)

where
k=1,...,p(\xi)
,
j=1,...,4
.

Proof.

We demonstrate the case
\xi\in[0,1)
, and the another case can be proved in similar way.
For
z\in\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}}
, when
1\leq q<+\infty
, by using definition of
V^{(2)}
and (
4.95
), we have

\displaystyle\parallel V^{(2)}-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}})}

\displaystyle=\parallel p_{11}(\xi_{1},\xi)T^{(i)}_{12}(\xi)(z-\xi_{1})^{-2i\nu(\xi_{1})}e^{2it\theta}\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\parallel e^{2it\theta}\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}})}.

For
z\in\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}}
, denote
z=\xi_{1}+se^{i\varphi}
,
s\in(\varrho,+\infty)
, by lemma
5
, we get

\displaystyle\parallel V^{(2)}-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}})}^{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}}}\exp\left(-qc(\xi)t\text{Im}z(\text{Re}^{2}z-\xi_{i}^{2})g(\text{Re}z;\xi)\right)dz

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\varrho}^{+\infty}\exp\left(-qc^{\prime}(\xi)ts\right)ds\lesssim t^{-1}\exp\left(-qc^{\prime}(\xi)t\varrho\right).

The second step is from
g(\text{Re}z;\xi)(\text{Re}^{2}z-\xi_{i}^{2})
has nonzero boundary on
\Sigma_{11}\setminus U_{\xi_{1}}
. And when
q=+\infty
is obviously.
For
z\in\Sigma_{kj}^{\prime}
, we only give the details of
\Sigma_{14}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\parallel V^{(2)}-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{14}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\parallel R_{14}(z)e^{-2it\theta}\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{14}^{\prime})}\lesssim\parallel e^{-2it\theta}\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{1+}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-1/q}\exp\left(-c^{\prime\prime}(\xi)t\right).

∎

This proposition means that the jump matrix
V^{(2)}(z)
uniformly goes to
I
on
\ddot{\Sigma}\setminus U(\xi)
.
therefore, outside of
U(\xi)
, there is only exponentially small error (in
t
) by completely ignoring the jump condition of
M^{R}(z)
.
And this proposition enlightens us to construct the solution
M^{R}(z)
as follows:

M^{R}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}E(z,\xi)M^{(r)}(z),&z\notin U(\xi)\cup\mathbb{B}_{j},\\
E(z,\xi)M^{(r)}(z)M^{lo}(z),&z\in U(\xi),\\
E(z,\xi)M^{(r)}(z)M^{B}_{j}(z),&z\in\mathbb{B}_{j}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.135)

Note that, when
\xi\in(1,+\infty)
,
M^{(r)}(z)
has no jump except the circle around poles not in
\lozenge
, and it has no phase point. So
U(\xi)=\emptyset
in these case, which means
M^{R}(z)=M^{(r)}(z)
. And it is more easy. On the other hand, for the case
\xi\in(-1/8,1)
, the definition above implies that
M^{lo}
is pole free and has no singularity. This construction decomposes
M^{R}
to two parts:
M^{(r)}
solves the pure RHP obtained by ignoring the jump conditions of RHP
4
, which is shown in Section
5
;
M^{lo}
uses parabolic cylinder functions to build a matrix to match jumps of
M^{(2)}
in a neighborhood of each critical point which is shown in Section
6
. And

M^{B}_{j}(z)
is a solution of a RHP which only has jump near
\varkappa_{j}
in Section
6.2
. Finally, as the error function,

E(z,\xi)
will be different in different case of
\xi
and is a solution of a small-norm Riemann-Hilbert problem shown in Section
6.3
.

We now use
M^{R}(z)
to construct a new matrix function

M^{(3)}(z;y,t)\triangleq M^{(3)}(z)=M^{(2)}(z)M^{R}(z)^{-1}.

(4.136)

which removes analytical component
M^{R}
to get a pure
\bar{\partial}
-problem.

\bar{\partial}
-problem
. Find a matrix-valued function
M^{(3)}(z;y,t)\triangleq M^{(3)}(z)
with following identities:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{(3)}(z)
is continuous and has sectionally continuous first partial derivatives in
\mathbb{C}
.

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behavior:

\displaystyle M^{(3)}(z)\sim I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty;

(4.137)

\blacktriangleright

\bar{\partial}
-Derivative: We have

\bar{\partial}M^{(3)}=M^{(3)}W^{(3)},\ \ z\in\mathbb{C},

where

W^{(3)}=M^{R}(z)\bar{\partial}R^{(2)}(z)M^{R}(z)^{-1}.

(4.138)

Proof.

By using properties of the solutions
M^{(2)}
and
M^{R}
for RHP
4
and
\bar{\partial}
-problem,
the analyticity is obtained immediately.
Since
M^{(2)}
and
M^{R}
achieve same jump matrix, we have

\displaystyle M_{-}^{(3)}(z)^{-1}M_{+}^{(3)}(z)

\displaystyle=M_{-}^{(2)}(z)^{-1}M_{-}^{R}(z)M_{+}^{R}(z)^{-1}M_{+}^{(2)}(z)

\displaystyle=M_{-}^{(2)}(z)^{-1}V^{(2)}(z)^{-1}M_{+}^{(2)}(z)=I,

which means
M^{(3)}
has no jumps and is continuously everywhere. We can also show that
M^{(3)}
has no pole.
For

\zeta_{n}
with
n\in\lozenge
, let
\mathcal{W}_{n}=T^{-1}(\zeta_{n})B_{n}T(\zeta_{n})
which appears in the left side of the
corresponding residue condition of RHP
3
and RHP
4
. We take
\zeta_{n}\in S_{1}
as an example. Then
we have the Laurent expansions in
z-\zeta_{n}

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(z)=\frac{\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M^{(2)}(z)}{z-\zeta_{n}}+a(\zeta_{n})+\mathcal{O}(z-\zeta_{n}),

(4.139)

with
a(\lambda)
is a constant matrix

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M^{(2)}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(0,\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}\frac{-C_{n}T_{21}(\zeta_{n})e^{it[\theta_{12}]_{n}}[M^{(2)}(\zeta_{n})]_{1}}{z-\zeta_{n}},0\right)=a(\zeta_{n})\mathcal{W}_{n},

then

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(z)=a(\zeta_{n})\left[\dfrac{\mathcal{W}_{n}}{z-\zeta_{n}}+I\right]+\mathcal{O}(z-\zeta_{n}).

(4.140)

Similarly, for
M^{R}(z)^{-1}
,

\displaystyle M^{R}(z)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left[\dfrac{\mathcal{W}_{n}}{z-\zeta_{n}}+I\right]^{-1}a(\zeta_{n})^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(z-\zeta_{n})

\displaystyle=\left[\dfrac{-\mathcal{W}_{n}}{z-\zeta_{n}}+I\right]a(\zeta_{n})^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(z-\zeta_{n}).

(4.141)

Then

\displaystyle M^{(3)}(z)

\displaystyle=\left\{a(\zeta_{n})\left[\dfrac{\mathcal{W}}{z-\lambda}+I\right]\right\}\left\{\left[\dfrac{-\mathcal{W}}{z-\lambda}+I\right]a(\zeta_{n})^{-1}\right\}+\mathcal{O}(z-\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(1),

which implies that
M^{(3)}(z)
has removable singularities at
\zeta_{n}
. Similarly,
M^{(3)}(z)
has no singularities on
\varkappa_{l}=e^{\frac{i\pi(l-1)}{3}}
,
l=1,...,6
.
And the
\bar{\partial}
-derivative of
M^{(3)}(z)
comes from
M^{(3)}(z)
due to analyticity of
M^{R}(z)
.
∎

The unique existence and asymptotic of
M^{(3)}(z)
will be shown in section
6.3
.

5
Contribution from discrete spectrum

In this section, we build a reflectionless case of RHP
1
to show that its solution can be approximated with
M^{(r)}(z)
. As
V^{(2)}(z)\equiv 0
except on
\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}
, RHP
4
reduces to a the sectionally meromorphic function
M^{(r)}(z)
with jump discontinuities on the union of circles. Then, by relating
M^{(r)}(z)
with original RHP
1
, we show the existence and uniqueness of solution of the above RHP
4
.

5.1
M^{(r)}(z)
- and
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)
-solitons

Proposition 7
.

If
M^{(r)}(z)
is the solution of the RH problem
4
with scattering data
\mathcal{D}=\left\{r(z),\left\{\zeta_{n},C_{n}\right\}_{n\in\{1,...,6N_{0}\}}\right\}
,
then
M^{(r)}(z)
exists and is unique.

Proof.

To transform
M^{(r)}(z)
to the soliton-solution of RHP
1
, the jumps and poles need to be restored. We reverse the triangularity effected in (
3.92
) and (
4.104
):

N(z;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})=M^{(r)}(z)T^{-1}(z)G^{-1}(z)T(z)\tilde{\Pi}(z),

(5.1)

with
G(z)
defined in (
3.91
),
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}=\left\{r(z),\left\{\zeta_{n},C_{n}\delta_{\zeta_{n}}\right\}_{n\in\{1,...,6N_{0}\}}\right\}
and

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}(z)=\text{diag}\left(\frac{\Pi(z)\Pi^{A}(z)}{\Pi(\omega^{2}z)\Pi^{A}(\omega^{2}z)},\frac{\Pi(\omega z)\Pi^{A}(\omega z)}{\Pi(z)\Pi^{A}(z)},\frac{\Pi(\omega^{2}z)\Pi^{A}(\omega^{2}z)}{\Pi(\omega z)\Pi^{A}(\omega z)}\right).

(5.2)

Here, for
n\in\mathcal{N}=\{1,...,2N_{1}+N_{2}\}
,

\delta_{\zeta_{n}}=\frac{\delta_{3}(\zeta_{n})\delta_{5}(\zeta_{n})}{\delta_{1}^{2}(\zeta_{n})}=\frac{\delta_{1}(\omega^{2}\zeta_{n})\delta_{1}(\omega\zeta_{n})}{\delta_{1}^{2}(\zeta_{n})}.

Similarly, for
n\in\mathcal{N}=\{2N_{1}+N_{2},...,N_{0}\}
,

\delta_{\zeta_{n}}=\frac{\delta_{3}(\zeta_{n})\delta_{1}(\zeta_{n})}{\delta_{5}^{2}(\zeta_{n})}=\frac{\delta_{1}(\omega^{2}\zeta_{n})\delta_{1}(\zeta_{n})}{\delta_{1}^{2}(\omega\zeta_{n})}.

Then from the symmetry
\delta_{1}(z)^{-1}=\overline{\delta_{1}(\bar{z})}
,

\delta_{\zeta_{n+kN_{0}}}=\frac{\delta_{1}(\omega^{2}\zeta_{n+kN_{0}})\delta_{1}(\omega\zeta_{n+kN_{0}})}{\delta_{1}^{2}(\zeta_{n+kN_{0}})},

for
n\in\mathcal{N}=\{1,...,2N_{1}+N_{2}\}
,
k=1,...,5
. And

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta_{n+kN_{0}}}=\frac{\delta_{1}(\omega^{2}\zeta_{n+kN_{0}})\delta_{1}(\zeta_{n+kN_{0}})}{\delta_{1}^{2}(\omega\zeta_{n+kN_{0}})},

for
n\in\mathcal{N}=\{2N_{1}+N_{2},...,N_{0}\}
,
k=1,...,5
.
First we verify
N(z;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})
satisfying RHP
1
. This transformation to
N(z;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})
preserves the normalization conditions at the origin and infinity obviously. And comparing with (
3.92
), this transformation restores the jump on
\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}_{n}
to residue for
n\notin\lozenge
. As for
n\in\lozenge
, take
\zeta_{n}\in S_{1}
and
n\in\lozenge_{1}
as an example. Substitute (
4.131
) into the transformation:

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}N(z;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})=

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M^{(r)}(z)T^{-1}(z)G^{-1}(z)T(z)\tilde{\Pi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}N(z;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
C_{n}\delta_{\zeta_{n}}e^{-2it\theta_{n}}&0\end{array}\right).

Its analyticity and symmetry follow from the Proposition of
M^{(r)}(z)
,
T(z)
and
G(z)
immediately. Then
N(z;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})
is solution of RHP
1
with absence of reflection, whose exact solution exists and can be obtained as described similarly in Appendix A
[
33
]
. Its uniqueness
can be affirmed with the Liouville’s theorem. Then the uniqueness and existences of
M^{(r)}(z)
come from (
5.1
).
∎

The contribution to the
M^{(r)}(z)
comes from all discrete spectrum, some of which
are restored from the jump on
\partial\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{n}
and
\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}
. We will show
that these poles make very small contribution to the
M^{(r)}(z)
as
t\to\infty
.

We put all circles together and define

\partial\mathbb{D}=\cup_{n\in\mathcal{Z}\setminus\lozenge}(\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}\cup\partial\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{n}),

then we have the following estimate.

Lemma 6
.

For
1\leq q\leq+\infty
,
the jump matrix
V^{(2)}(z)\big|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}
given in (
4.106
)-(
4.107
) admits the estimate

\displaystyle\parallel V^{(2)}(z)-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\partial\mathbb{D})}=\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}),\ \ n\in\mathcal{Z}\setminus\lozenge.

(5.5)

where
\rho_{0}
is given by (
3.20
)

Proof.

Since
\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}
is bounded, we just need to prove that the estimate (
5.5
) for
L^{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{D}_{n})
is true.
Here take
z\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{n}
,
n\in\nabla_{1}
as an illustrative example.

\displaystyle\parallel V^{(2)}(z)-I\parallel_{L^{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{D}_{n})}=|C_{n}(z-\zeta_{n})^{-1}T_{21}(z)e^{it[\theta_{12}]_{n}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varrho^{-1}e^{\text{Re}(it[\theta_{12}]_{n})}\lesssim e^{-t\text{Im}([\theta_{12}]_{n})}\leq e^{-\delta_{0}t}.

∎

This Lemma inspires us to completely ignore the jump conditions on
\partial\mathbb{D}
, because they exponentially decay to
I
as
t\to\infty
.
The main contribution to
M^{(r)}(z)
comes from discrete spectrum
\left\{\zeta_{n}\right\}_{n\in\lozenge}
.
Let
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=M^{(r)}(z)\big|_{V^{(2)}(z)\equiv 0}
, then the RHP
4

reduces to the following RH problem.

RHP 5
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{\zeta_{n}\right\}_{n\in\lozenge}
;

\blacktriangleright
Symmetry:
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}

and
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=\overline{M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(\bar{z}^{-1})}
;

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=

\displaystyle I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty;

(5.6)

\blacktriangleright
Singularities: As
z\rightarrow\varkappa_{l}=e^{\frac{i\pi(l-1)}{3}},l=1,...,6
, the limit of
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)
have

pole singularities

\displaystyle M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp 1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm 1,

\displaystyle M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega^{2},

\displaystyle M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=\frac{1}{z\mp\omega}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&-\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\\
0&0&0\\
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}&\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}&\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(1),\ z\to\pm\omega,

with
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}=\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\alpha}^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}=\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}(y,t)=-\bar{\beta}^{(2)}_{\pm}
and
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)^{-1}
has same specific
matrix structure with
\alpha^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\beta^{(2)}_{\pm}
replaced by
\tilde{\alpha}^{(2)}_{\pm}
,
\tilde{\beta}^{(2)}_{\pm}
;

\blacktriangleright
Residue conditions:
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}
has simple poles at each point
\zeta_{n}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{n}
for
n\in\lozenge
with:

\displaystyle\res_{z=\zeta_{n}}M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)=\lim_{z\to\zeta_{n}}M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)\left[T^{-1}(z)B_{n}T(z)\right].

(5.16)

By using the Proposition
7
, we then have

Proposition 8
.

The RHP
5
exists an unique solution. The
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)
is equivalent to a solution of the original reflectionless RHP
1
with modified scattering data
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\lozenge}=\left\{0,\left\{\zeta_{n},C_{n}^{\lozenge}\right\}_{n\in\lozenge}\right\}
with

C_{n}^{\lozenge}=C_{n}T^{2}(\zeta_{n})
.

When
r(s)\equiv 0
, denote
u^{r}(x,t;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})
as the
\mathcal{N}(\lozenge)
-soliton with scattering data
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\lozenge}
.
By the reconstruction formula (
2.118
) and (
2.119
), we then have

Corollary 3
.

The soliton solution of the Novikov equation (
1.8
) is given by

\displaystyle u^{r}(x,t;\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\lozenge})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\tilde{m}^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{1}(y,t)\left(\frac{[M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{33}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}{[M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{11}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}[\tilde{m}^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{3}(y,t)\left(\frac{[M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{33}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}{[M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{11}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}\right)^{-1/2}-1,

(5.17)

in which

\displaystyle x(y,t;\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\lozenge})=y+c^{r}_{+}(x,t;\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\lozenge})=y+\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{M_{33}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)}{M_{11}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t)},

(5.18)

\displaystyle[\tilde{m}^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{j}\triangleq\sum_{n=1}^{3}[M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}]_{nj}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}};y,t),\ j=1,2,3.

5.2
Residual error between
M^{(r)}
- and
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}
-solitons

To obtain the residual error between
M^{(r)}
and
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}
, we make the factorization

M^{(r)}(z)=\tilde{E}(z)M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z),

(5.19)

where
\tilde{E}(z)
is a error function, which can be obtained by solving
the following small-norm RH problem

RHP 6
.

Find a matrix-valued function
\tilde{E}(z)
with following properties

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
\tilde{E}(z)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\partial\mathbb{D}
;

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle\tilde{E}(z)\sim I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636pt|z|\rightarrow\infty;

(5.20)

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
\tilde{E}(z)
has continuous boundary values
\tilde{E}_{\pm}(z)
on
\partial\mathbb{D}
satisfying

\tilde{E}_{+}(z)=\tilde{E}_{-}(z)V^{\tilde{E}}(z),

where the jump matrix
V^{\tilde{E}}(z)
is given by

V^{\tilde{E}}(z)=M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)V^{(2)}(z)M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)^{-1}.

(5.21)

Proposition
8
shows that
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)
is bound on
\Sigma^{(2)}
. By using Lemma
6
, we have the following uniformly decaying
evaluate

\parallel V^{\tilde{E}}(z)-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\partial\mathbb{D})}\lesssim\parallel V^{(2)}-I\parallel_{L^{q}(\partial\mathbb{D})}=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}).

(5.22)

Therefore,
the existence and uniqueness of the RHP
6
is shown by using a small-norm RH problem
[
22
,
23
]
, moreover its solution can be given by

\tilde{E}(z)=I+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\dfrac{\eta(s)(V^{\tilde{E}}-I)}{s-z}ds,

(5.23)

where
\eta\in L^{2}(\partial\mathbb{D})
is a unique solution of Fredholm equation

(1-C_{\tilde{E}})\eta=I.

(5.24)

The integral operator
C_{\tilde{E}}
:
L^{2}\to L^{2}
is given by

C_{\tilde{E}}(\eta)=\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(\eta(V^{\tilde{E}}-I)\right),

where
\mathcal{P}^{-}
is a Plemelj projection operator

\mathcal{P}^{-}(\eta(V^{\tilde{E}}-I))=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\dfrac{\eta(V^{\tilde{E}}-I)}{s-(z-i\varepsilon)}ds.

(5.25)

Then by (
5.21
), we have

\parallel C_{\tilde{E}}\parallel_{L^{2}}\leq\parallel\mathcal{P}^{-}\parallel_{L^{2}}\parallel V^{\tilde{E}}-I\parallel_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}),

(5.26)

which means
\parallel C_{\tilde{E}}\parallel_{L^{2}}<1
for sufficiently large
t
, so
\eta
uniquely exists and

\parallel\eta\parallel_{L^{2}}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}).

(5.27)

In order to reconstruct the solution
u(x,t)
of the Novikov equation (
1.8
), we need the asymptotic
of
\tilde{E}(z)
as
z\to\infty
and the long time asymptotic of
\tilde{E}(i)
.

Proposition 9
.

The residual error
\tilde{E}(z)
defined by (
5.23
) admits estimate

|\tilde{E}(z)-I|\lesssim\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}),\ t\to\infty.

Moreover
\tilde{E}(z)
has expansion at
z=e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{E}(z)=\tilde{E}(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}})+\tilde{E}_{1}(z-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}})+\mathcal{O}((z-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}})^{2}),

(5.28)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{E}(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}})=I+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Sigma^{(2)}}\dfrac{\eta(s)(V^{\tilde{E}}-I)}{s-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}}}ds,

(5.29)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{1}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Sigma^{(2)}}\frac{\eta(s)(V^{\tilde{E}}-I)}{(s-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}})^{2}}ds,

which satisfy the following estimates

|\tilde{E}(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}})-I|\lesssim\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}),\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{E}_{1}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}).

(5.30)

Proof.

By combining (
5.27
) and (
5.22
), we obtain

|\tilde{E}(z)-I|\leq|(1-C_{\tilde{E}})(\eta)|+|C_{\tilde{E}}(\eta)|\lesssim\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}).

(5.31)

The estimate
\tilde{E}(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}})
in (
5.30
) is obtained by taking
z=e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}}
in above estimate.
As
z\to e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}}
, geometrically expanding
(s-z)^{-1}

for
z
large in (
5.23
) leads to (
5.28
). Finally for
\tilde{E}_{1}
, noting that
|s-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}}|^{-2}
is bounded on
\Sigma^{(2)}
, then

\displaystyle|\tilde{E}_{1}|\lesssim\parallel\eta\parallel_{2}\parallel V^{\tilde{E}}-I\parallel_{2}\lesssim\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}).

∎

Corollary 4
.

Assume that
M^{(r)}(z)(z)
and
M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)
are two solutions of the RHP
5
and
RHP
4
respectively, then they have the relation

M^{(r)}(z)(z)=M^{(r)}_{\lozenge}(z)\left[I+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t})\right],\ t\to\infty.

(5.32)

The corresponding soliton solutions have the relation

u^{r}(x,t;\tilde{\mathcal{D}})=u^{r}(x,t;\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\lozenge})+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\min\{\rho_{0},\delta_{0}\}t}).

(5.33)

6
Contribution from jump contours

6.1
Local solvable RH problem near phase points

When
\xi\in(-1/8,1)
, proposition
6
gives that out of
U(\xi)
, the jumps are exponentially close to the identity.
Hence we need to continue our investigation near the stationary phase points in this section.

We define a new local contour

\Sigma^{lo}={\Sigma}^{lo}_{\omega^{0}}\cup{\Sigma}^{lo}_{\omega^{1}}\cup{\Sigma}^{lo}_{\omega^{2}},

where
{\Sigma}^{lo}_{\omega^{n}},\ n=0,1,2
are the local contours on jump contours
\omega^{n}\mathbb{R}
, respectively

\displaystyle{\Sigma}^{lo}_{\omega^{n}}=(\underset{k=1,..,p(\xi)}{\underset{j=1,...,4,}{\cup}}\Sigma_{kj}^{\omega^{n}})\cap U(\xi),\ n=0,1,2.

Since there are
3p(\xi)
phase points
\xi_{n,k}=\omega^{n}\xi_{k},n=0,1,2;k=1,\cdots,p(\xi)
, so
the local jump contour
\Sigma^{lo}
is consist of
3p(\xi)
crosses with
p(\xi)=4
for the case
0\leq\xi<1

and
p(\xi)=8
for the case
-{1}/{8}<\xi<0
. See Figure
10
.
Further denote the local jump cross for each phase point
\xi_{n,k}

\displaystyle\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}=(\underset{j=1,...,4}{\cup}\Sigma_{kj}^{\omega^{n}})\cap U(\xi),\ n=0,1,2;\ k=1,\cdots,p(\xi),

\displaystyle\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}\cap\Sigma^{lo,n}_{j}=\varnothing,k\not=j.

We consider the following local RH problem

z

Figure (a) is the local jump contour

;

RHP 7
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{lo}(z)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{lo}(z)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{lo}
;

\blacktriangleright
Symmetry:
M^{lo}(z)=\Gamma_{1}\overline{M^{lo}(\bar{z})}\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M^{lo}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M^{lo}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}

and
M^{lo}(z)=\overline{M^{lo}(\bar{z}^{-1})}
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
M^{lo}
has continuous boundary values
M^{lo}_{\pm}
on
\Sigma
and

M^{lo}_{+}(z)=M^{lo}_{-}(z)V^{lo}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\Sigma^{lo},

(6.1)

where
V^{lo}(z)=V^{(2)}(z)\big|_{\Sigma^{lo}}
.

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{lo}(z)=

\displaystyle I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty.

(6.2)

This local RH problem, which consists of
3p(\xi)
local models on
\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}
about
phase point
\xi_{n,k}
, has the jump condition and no poles.
First, we show as
t\to\infty
, the interaction between
\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}
and
\Sigma^{lo,n}_{j}
reduces to 0 to higher order and the contribution to
the solution of
M^{lo}(z)
is simply the sum of the separate contributions from
\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}
.

We consider the trivial decomposition of the jump matrix

\displaystyle V^{lo}(z)=b_{-}^{-1}(z)b_{+}(z),\ \ \;b_{-}(z)=I,\ \ \;b_{+}(z)=V^{lo}(z),

\displaystyle w_{-}(z)=0,\ \ w_{+}(z)=V^{lo}(z)-I,\ \ w(z)=V^{lo}(z)-I.

Define

\displaystyle w_{k}^{n}(z)=w(z)\big|_{\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}},\ \ w_{k,\pm}^{n}(z)=w_{\pm}(z)\big|_{\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}},

\displaystyle V^{lo,n}_{k}(z)=V^{lo}(z)|_{\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}},\ \ w_{k}^{n}(z)=0,\ z\in\Sigma^{lo,n}_{j},j\not=k.

which determine a local model with the jump contour
\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}
at
phase point
\xi_{n,k}

RHP 8
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{lo,n}_{k}(z)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{lo,n}_{k}(z)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
M^{lo,n}_{k}(z)
has continuous boundary values
M^{lo,n}_{k,\pm}
and

M^{lo,n}_{k,+}(z)=M^{lo,n}_{k,-}(z)V^{lo,n}_{k}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}.

(6.3)

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{lo,n}_{k}(z)=

\displaystyle I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty.

(6.4)

By using above factorization,
w(z)
can be written as

\displaystyle w=\underset{n=0,1,2}{\underset{k=1,..,p(\xi)}{\Sigma}}w_{k}^{n}(z),\ \ \ k=1,...,p(\xi),n=0,1,2.

and the Cauchy operators are given by

\displaystyle C_{w}(f)=\mathcal{P}^{-}(fw_{+}),\hskip 14.22636ptC_{w_{k}^{n}}(f)=\mathcal{P}^{-}(fw_{k,+}^{n}),\ \ C_{w}=\underset{n=0,1,2}{\underset{k=1,..,p(\xi)}{\Sigma}}C_{w_{k}^{n}},

(6.5)

where
\mathcal{P}^{-}
is the Cauchy projection operator defined by

\mathcal{P}^{-}(f)(z)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Sigma^{lo}/\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}}\dfrac{f(s)}{s-(z-i\varepsilon)}ds,\ \ z\in\Sigma^{lo}/\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k},

(6.6)

A simple calculation gives

Lemma 7
.

The matrix functions
w(z)
and
w_{k}^{n}(z)
defined above admits following estimate

\displaystyle\parallel w(z)\parallel_{L^{q}(\Sigma^{lo})},\ \ \parallel w_{k}^{n}(z)\parallel_{L^{p}(\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k})}=\mathcal{O}(t^{-1/2}),\ 1\leq q<+\infty.

(6.7)

This lemma implies that
I-C_{w}
and
I-C_{w_{k}^{n}}
are reversible, so both RHP
7
and RHP
8
exists a unique solution and can be written as

\displaystyle M^{lo}=I+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Sigma^{lo}}\frac{(I-C_{w})^{-1}I\ w}{s-z}ds.

(6.8)

Then following the step of
[
21
]
, we can directly derive that

Proposition 10
.

As
t\to+\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\parallel C_{w_{k}^{n}}C_{w_{j}^{n}}\parallel_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim t^{-1},\hskip 14.22636pt\parallel C_{w_{k}^{n}}C_{w_{j}^{n}}\parallel_{L^{\infty}\to L^{2}}\lesssim t^{-1}.

(6.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma^{lo}}\frac{(I-C_{w})^{-1}I\ w}{s-z}ds=\underset{n=0,1,2}{\underset{k=1,..,p(\xi)}{\Sigma}}\int_{\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}}\frac{(I-C_{w_{k}^{n}})^{-1}I\ w_{k}^{n}}{s-z}ds+\mathcal{O}(t^{-3/2}).

(6.10)

This Proposition implies that
contributions of every cross
\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}
can be separated out, and the solution
M^{lo}
of the RHP
7

can be given by the sum of the local RHP
8
of all phase points
\xi_{n,k}
.
We further show that the solution of each local model can be given explicitly in terms of parabolic cylinder
functions on every contour
\Sigma^{lo,n}_{k}
when
t\to\infty
.
As illustrative example, we only consider a local model at phase point
\xi_{0,1}:=\xi_{1}
in the RHP
8
, its jump cross
denotes (red cross in Figure
10
)

\Sigma^{lo}_{1}=(\underset{j=1,...,4}{\cup}\Sigma_{1j})\cap U(\xi)=\{z=\xi_{1}+le^{\pm\varphi i},\ l\in\mathbb{R}\},

which corresponds to the following local RH problem

RHP 9
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{lo,1}(z)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{lo,1}(z)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{lo}_{1}
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
M^{lo,1}
has continuous boundary values
M^{lo,1}_{\pm}
and

M^{lo,1}_{+}(z)=M^{lo,1}_{-}(z)V^{lo,1}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\Sigma^{lo}_{1},

(6.11)

where when
|z-\xi_{1}|\leq\varrho^{(0)}
, for the case
\xi\in(-1/8,0)
,

\displaystyle V^{lo,1}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&r(\xi_{1})T_{12}^{(1)}(\xi)^{-1}\left(\eta(z-\xi_{1})\right)^{-2i\eta\nu(\xi_{i})}e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Sigma_{11}\cap U(\xi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
-\bar{r}(\xi_{1})T_{12}^{(1)}(\xi)\left(\eta(z-\xi_{1})\right)^{2i\eta\nu(\xi_{1})}e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Sigma_{12}\cap U(\xi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{r(\xi_{1})}{1-|r(\xi_{1})|^{2}}T_{12}^{(1)}(\xi)^{-1}\left(\eta(z-\xi_{1})\right)^{-2i\eta\nu(\xi_{i})}e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Sigma_{13}\cap U(\xi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\frac{-\bar{r}(\xi_{1})}{1-|r(\xi_{1})|^{2}}T_{12}^{(1)}(\xi)\left(\eta(z-\xi_{1})\right)^{2\eta i\nu(\xi_{1})}e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\Sigma_{14}\cap U(\xi).\end{array}\right.

For the case
\xi\in(0,1)
, the corresponding jump matrix
V^{lo,1}(z)
from above (
9
)
by exchanging
\Sigma_{11}\longleftrightarrow\Sigma_{14},\ \ \Sigma_{12}\longleftrightarrow\Sigma_{13}
.

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{lo,1}(z)=

\displaystyle I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty.

(6.24)

In near
\xi_{1}
, we rewrite phase function as

\displaystyle\theta_{12}(z)=\theta_{12}(\xi_{1})+(z-\xi_{1})^{2}\theta_{12}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{1})+\mathcal{O}((z-\xi_{1})^{3}).

(6.25)

For
\xi\in[0,1)
,
\theta_{12}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{1})>0
and when
\xi\in(-1/8,0)
,
\theta_{12}^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{1})<0
.
Recall the
\eta(\xi,j)
defined in (
3.2
).

The RHP
9
is a local model and does not possess the symmetry.
In order to motivate the model, let
\zeta=\zeta(z)
denote the rescaled local variable

\displaystyle\zeta(z)=t^{1/2}\sqrt{-4\eta(\xi,1)\theta^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{1})}(z-\xi_{1}),

(6.26)

where
\eta(\xi,1)=1
as
\xi\in(-1/8,0)
and
\eta(\xi,1)=-1
as
\xi\in[0,1)
in (
3.2
).
This change of variable maps
U_{\xi_{1}}
to an neighborhood of
\zeta=0
. Additionally, let

\displaystyle r_{\xi_{1}}=r(\xi_{1})T_{1}(\xi)^{2}e^{-2it\theta(\xi_{1})}\eta^{-2i\eta\nu(\xi_{1})}\exp\left\{-i\eta\nu(\xi_{1})\log\left(4t\theta^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{1})\tilde{\eta}(\xi_{1})\right)\right\},

(6.27)

with
|r_{\xi_{1}}|=|r(\xi_{1})|
.
In the above expression, the complex powers are defined by choosing the branch of
the logarithm with
-\pi<\arg\zeta<\pi
in the cases
\xi\in(-1/8,0)
, and the branch of the logarithm with
0<\arg\zeta<2\pi
in the case
\xi\in[0,1)
.

Through this change of variable, the jump matrix
V^{lo,1}(z)
approximates to the jump matrix of a parabolic cylinder model problem as follows.

RHP 10
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{pc}(\zeta;\xi)

and in the case

\displaystyle V^{pc}(\zeta;\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\frac{-\bar{r}_{\xi_{1}}}{1-|r_{\xi_{1}}|^{2}}\zeta^{-2i\nu(\xi_{1})}e^{-\frac{i}{2}\zeta^{2}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}e^{\varphi i},\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{r_{\xi_{1}}}{1-|r_{\xi_{1}}|^{2}}\zeta^{2i\nu(\xi_{1})}e^{\frac{i}{2}\zeta^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}e^{(2\pi-\varphi)i},\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
-\bar{r}_{\xi_{1}}\zeta^{-2i\nu(\xi_{1})}e^{\frac{i}{2}\zeta^{2}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}e^{(\pi+\varphi)i},\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&r_{\xi_{1}}\zeta^{2i\nu(\xi_{1})}e^{-\frac{i}{2}\zeta^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}e^{(\pi-\varphi)i}.\end{array}\right.

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{pc}(\zeta;\xi)=

\displaystyle M^{lo,1}(z)=I+\frac{t^{-1/2}}{z-\xi_{1}}\frac{i\tilde{\eta}}{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&[M^{pc}_{1}]_{12}&0\\
-[M^{pc}_{1}]_{21}&0&0\\

The RHP
10
has an explicit solution
M^{pc}(\zeta)
, which is expressed in terms of solutions of the parabolic cylinder equation
\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z^{2}}{2}+a\right)\right)D_{a}(z)=0
. In fact, let

\displaystyle M^{pc}(\zeta;\xi)=\Psi(\zeta;\xi)P(\xi)e^{-\frac{i}{4}\eta\zeta^{2}\sigma_{3}}\zeta^{-i\eta\nu(\xi_{1})\sigma_{3}},

(6.59)

where in the case
\xi\in[0,1)

\displaystyle P(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
-\frac{\bar{r}_{\xi_{1}}}{1-|r_{\xi_{1}}|^{2}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(0,\varphi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-\frac{r_{\xi_{1}}}{1-|r_{\xi_{1}}|^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(2\pi-\varphi,2\pi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\bar{r}_{\xi_{1}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(\pi,\pi+\varphi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&r_{\xi_{1}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(\pi-\varphi,\pi),\\[10.0pt]
I,&else.\end{array}\right.

and in the case

\displaystyle P(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&r_{\xi_{1}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(0,\varphi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\bar{r}_{\xi_{1}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(-\varphi,0),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-\frac{r_{\xi_{1}}}{1-|r_{\xi_{1}}|^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(-\pi+\varphi,-\pi),\\[10.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\

0&0&1\end{array}\right),&\arg\zeta\in(\pi-\varphi,\pi),\\[10.0pt]
I,&else.\end{array}\right.

And

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right),\ with\ a^{\sigma_{3}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}a&0&0\\

By construction, the matrix
\Psi
is continuous along the rays of
\Sigma^{pc}
. And Due to the branch cut of the logarithmic function along
\tilde{\eta}\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the matrix
\Psi
has the same (constant) jump matrix along the negative and positive real axis. The function
\Psi(\zeta;\xi)
satisfies the following model RHP.

RHP 11
.

Find a matrix-valued function
\Psi(\zeta;\xi)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
\Psi(\zeta;\xi)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
\Psi(\zeta;\xi)
has continuous boundary values
\Psi_{\pm}(\zeta;\xi)
on
\mathbb{R}
and

\Psi_{+}(\zeta;\xi)=\Psi_{-}(\zeta;\xi)V^{\Psi}(\zeta),\hskip 14.22636pt\zeta\in\mathbb{R},

(6.92)

where

\displaystyle V^{\Psi}(\xi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-r_{\xi_{1}}&0\\
\bar{r}_{\xi_{1}}&1-|r_{\xi_{1}}|^{2}&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right).

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle\Psi(\zeta;\xi)\sim

\displaystyle\left(I+M^{pc}_{1}\zeta^{-1}\right)\zeta^{i\eta\nu(\xi_{1})\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{i}{4}\eta\zeta^{2}\sigma_{3}},\hskip 14.22636pt\zeta\rightarrow\infty.

(6.96)

For brevity, denote
\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{12}=i\eta[M^{pc}_{1}]_{12}
,
\tilde{\beta}^{1}=-i\eta[M^{pc}_{1}]_{21}

. The unique solution to Problem

1.
\xi\in[0,1)
,
when

\displaystyle\Psi(\zeta;\xi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{\frac{3}{4}\pi\nu_{1}}D_{-i\nu_{1}}(e^{-\frac{3}{4}\pi i}\zeta)&\frac{i\nu_{1}}{\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{21}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{4}(\nu_{1}+i)}D_{i\nu_{1}-1}(e^{-\frac{\pi i}{4}}\zeta)&0\\
-\frac{i\nu_{1}}{\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{12}}e^{\frac{3\pi}{4}(\nu_{1}-i)}D_{-i\nu_{1}-1}(e^{-\frac{3\pi i}{4}}\zeta)&e^{-\frac{\pi}{4}\nu_{1}}D_{i\nu_{1}}(e^{-\frac{\pi}{4}i}\zeta)&0\\

\frac{i\nu_{1}}{\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{12}}e^{\frac{3\pi}{4}(\nu_{1}+i)}D_{i\nu_{1}-1}(e^{\frac{3\pi i}{4}}\zeta)&e^{-\frac{\pi}{4}\nu_{1}}D_{-i\nu_{1}}(e^{\frac{\pi}{4}i}\zeta)&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right).

And when

,

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{21}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\frac{5}{2}\pi\nu_{1}}e^{-\frac{7\pi}{4}i}}{r_{\xi_{1}}\Gamma(-i\nu_{1})},\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{21}\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{12}=-\nu_{1},

(6.109)

\displaystyle|\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{21}|=-\frac{\nu_{1}}{(1-|r(\xi_{1})|^{2})^{3}},

(6.110)

(6.112)

(6.113)

\displaystyle M^{lo,1}(z)=I+\frac{t^{-1/2}}{z-\xi_{1}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\tilde{\beta}^{1}_{12}&0\\

For the model around other stationary phase points, it also admits

.

\displaystyle r_{\xi_{k}}=r(\xi_{k})T_{k}(\xi)^{2}e^{-2it\theta(\xi_{k})}\exp\left\{-i\nu(\xi_{k})\log\left(4t\theta^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{k})\right)\right\},

(6.120)

(6.123)

(6.127)

Then together with proposition
10
, wo final obtain

Proposition 12
.

As

,

\displaystyle M^{lo}(z)=I+t^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p(\xi)}\left(\frac{A_{k}(\xi)}{z-\xi_{k}}+\frac{\omega\Gamma_{3}\overline{A_{k}(\xi)}\Gamma_{3}}{z-\omega\xi_{k}}+\frac{\omega^{2}\Gamma_{2}\overline{A_{k}(\xi)}\Gamma_{2}}{z-\omega^{2}\xi_{k}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1}),

\tilde{\beta}^{k}_{21}&0&0\\

6.2
RH problem near singularities
\varkappa_{j}

The matrix function
M^{B}_{j}(z)
is the solution of following RH problem

RHP 12
.

Find a matrix-valued function
M^{B}_{j}(z)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
M^{B}_{j}(z)
is meromorphic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\mathbb{B}_{j}\cap l_{j}\right)
;

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
M^{B}_{j}
has continuous boundary values
[M^{B}_{j}]_{\pm}
on
\mathbb{B}_{j}\cap l_{j}
and

[M^{B}_{j}]_{+}(z)=[M^{B}_{j}]_{-}(z)V^{(2)}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in\mathbb{B}_{j}\cap l_{j};

(6.132)

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle M^{B}_{j}(z)=

\displaystyle I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\rightarrow\infty;

(6.133)

By the symmetry of
M^{R}
, we obtain that
M^{B}_{3}(z)=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M^{B}_{1}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}
for
z\in\mathbb{B}_{3}
;
M^{B}_{5}(z)=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M^{B}_{1}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}
for
z\in\mathbb{B}_{5}
and
M^{B}_{6}(z)=\Gamma_{3}\overline{M^{B}_{4}(\omega\bar{z})}\Gamma_{3}
for
z\in\mathbb{B}_{6}
;
M^{B}_{2}(z)=\Gamma_{2}\overline{M^{B}_{4}(\omega^{2}\bar{z})}\Gamma_{2}
for
z\in\mathbb{B}_{2}
. Thus, it is reasonably to only give the detail of
M^{B}_{1}(z)
. Then
M^{B}_{4}(z)
can be obtained analogously and the others can be obtained by symmetry.
M^{B}_{1}(z)
only has jump
V^{b}
on
(1-2\varepsilon,1+2\varepsilon)
:

\displaystyle V^{B}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{rT_{21}\mathcal{X}e^{it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\frac{\bar{r}T_{12}\mathcal{X}e^{-it\theta_{12}}}{1-|r|^{2}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right).

To get the long-time behavior of
M^{B}_{1}(e^{\frac{\pi i}{6}})
, we trans it to a pure
\bar{\partial}
-problem by multiplying the function
R^{(B)}
defined as follow:

\displaystyle R^{(B)}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&R^{(B)}_{+}e^{it\theta_{12}}&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\mathbb{C}^{-};\\[12.0pt]
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
R^{(B)}_{-}e^{-it\theta_{12}}&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),&z\in\mathbb{C}^{+};\end{array}\right.

Here,

\displaystyle R^{(B)}_{+}(z)=\mathcal{X}(\text{Re}z)\mathcal{X}(\text{Im}z+1)f(\text{Re}z)g(z),

(6.134)

\displaystyle R^{(B)}_{-}(z)=\overline{R^{(B)}_{+}(\bar{z})},

(6.135)

with

\displaystyle f(z)=\frac{r(z)}{1-|r(z)|^{2}},\hskip 14.22636ptg(z)=T_{12}(z).

(6.136)

Then by simply calculation, we have

\displaystyle|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}_{+}|\lesssim|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\text{Re}z)\mathcal{X}(\text{Im}z+1)|+|\mathcal{X}(\text{Re}z)\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\text{Im}z+1)|.

(6.137)

Obviously, the support of
R^{(B)}_{+}
and
\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}_{+}
are contained in
\mathbb{B}_{1}
.
Denote

\displaystyle\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}=M^{B}_{1}R^{(B)},

(6.138)

with

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}=M^{B}_{1}\bar{\partial}R^{(B)},\ \ \ \tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(z)\sim I,\ z\to\infty.

(6.139)

Specially,
\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}
has no jump. Therefore, it is solution of the integral equation

\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(z)=I+\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\dfrac{\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(s)\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)}{s-z}dm(s).

(6.140)

Denote
C^{B}
is the integral operator:
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
as

C^{B}f(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\dfrac{f(s)\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)}{s-z}dm(s).

(6.141)

Proposition 13
.

C^{B}
is a bounded integral operator from
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
with:

\displaystyle\parallel C^{B}\parallel\lesssim t^{-1/p},\ p>1.

(6.142)

It implies that when
t\to\infty
,
\left(1-C^{B}\right)^{-1}
exists.

Proof.

Obviously,

\displaystyle\parallel C^{B}\parallel\lesssim\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{+}}\dfrac{|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|}{|s-z|}dm(s)+\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{-}}\dfrac{|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|}{|s-z|}dm(s).

(6.143)

Take the first term as an example. Let
s=u+vi=le^{i\vartheta}
,
z=x+yi
. In the following computation, we will use the inequality

\displaystyle\parallel|s-z|^{-1}\parallel_{L^{q}(0,+\infty)}

\displaystyle=\left\{\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left[\left(\frac{u-x}{v-y}\right)^{2}+1\right]^{-q/2}d\left(\frac{u-x}{|v-y|}\right)\right\}^{1/q}|v-y|^{1/q-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim|v-y|^{1/q-1},

(6.144)

with
1<q<+\infty
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Thus, by either Lemma
1
or Corollary
2
, we both have

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{+}}\dfrac{|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|}{|s-z|}dm(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\varepsilon}\int_{1-2\varepsilon}^{1+2\varepsilon}|s-z|^{-1}|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|due^{-c(\xi)v}dv

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{2\varepsilon}v^{-1/q}e^{-c(\xi)v}dv\lesssim t^{-1/p}.

(6.145)

∎

Hence, from
\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(z)=\left(1-C^{B}\right)^{-1}\dot{I}
, we get the existence and uniqueness of
\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(z)
. Take
z=e^{\frac{\pi i}{6}}
in (
6.140
), then

\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(z)-I=\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\dfrac{\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(s)\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)}{s-e^{\frac{\pi i}{6}}}dm(s).

(6.146)

Proposition 14
.

There exists a constant
T_{1}
, such that for all
t>T_{1}
,
\tilde{M}^{B}(z)
admits the following estimate

\displaystyle\parallel\tilde{M}^{B}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}})-I\parallel\lesssim t^{-1}.

(6.147)

Proof.

Because
e^{\frac{\pi i}{6}}\notin\mathbb{B}_{1}
,
|s-e^{\frac{\pi i}{6}}|\lesssim 1
in
\mathbb{B}_{1}
. So

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\dfrac{\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(s)\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)}{s-e^{\frac{\pi i}{6}}}dm(s)\lesssim\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{+}}|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|dm(s)+\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{-}}|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|dm(s).

Similarly, we take the first term as an example

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{C}^{+}}|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|dm(s)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{2\varepsilon}\int_{1-2\varepsilon}^{1+2\varepsilon}|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|due^{-c(\xi)v}dv

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{2\varepsilon}e^{-c(\xi)v}dv\lesssim t^{-1}.

(6.148)

∎

Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle M^{B}_{1}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}})=\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}})R^{(B)}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}})=I+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1}).

(6.149)

Then for
j=1,...,6
,

\displaystyle M^{B}_{j}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}})=I+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1}).

(6.150)

In addition, on the boundary of
\mathbb{B}_{1}
,
R^{(B)}(z)=I
, so
M^{B}_{1}(z)=\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(z)
on it. In fact, for
z\in\partial\mathbb{B}
, when
s\in\partial\mathbb{B}
, we still have
\dfrac{|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|}{|s-z|}=0
. It implies that
\dfrac{|\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(s)|}{|s-z|}
is bounded for
z\in\partial\mathbb{B}
,
s\in\mathbb{B}
. Then similar as in above Proposition, we have for
z\in\partial\mathbb{B}
,

\displaystyle M^{B}_{j}(z)=I+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1}).

(6.151)

r(\pm 1)=0
, gives that
\bar{\partial}R^{(B)}(\pm 1)=I
, so
M^{B}_{1}(1)=\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(1)=I+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1/p})
. Here,
p>1
is a arbitrary constant. So we can denote
p
as
1/p=1-\rho
with a small enough positive constant
\rho<\frac{1}{4}
. Then
M^{B}_{1}(1)=\tilde{M}^{B}_{1}(1)=I+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1+\rho})
. Further, we have for
l=1,...,6
,

\displaystyle M^{B}_{l}(\kappa_{l})=\tilde{M}^{B}_{l}(\kappa_{l})=I+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1+\rho}).

(6.152)

6.3
Small norm RH problem for residual error

In this section, we consider the error matrix-function
E(z;\xi)
.
From the definition (
4.135
), we can obtain a RH problem for the
E(z;\xi)
:

RHP 13
.

Find a matrix-valued function
E(z;\xi)
with following properties:

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
E(z;\xi)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{E}(\xi)
, where

\displaystyle\Sigma^{E}(\xi)=\cup_{j=1}^{6}\partial\mathbb{B}_{j},\ \ \xi\in(-\infty,-1/8)\cup(1,+\infty),

\displaystyle\Sigma^{E}(\xi)=\left(\cup_{j=1}^{6}\partial\mathbb{B}_{j}\right)\cup\partial U(\xi)\cup(\Sigma^{(2)}\setminus U(\xi)),\ \ \xi\in(-1/8,1).

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle E(z;\xi)\sim I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636pt|z|\rightarrow\infty;

(6.153)

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
E(z;\xi)
has continuous boundary values
E_{\pm}(z;\xi)
satisfying

E_{+}(z;\xi)=E_{-}(z;\xi)V^{E}(z),\ \ z\in\Sigma^{E}(\xi),

where the jump matrix
V^{E}(z)
is given by

V^{E}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}M^{(r)}(z)V^{(2)}(z)M^{(r)}(z)^{-1},&z\in\Sigma^{(2)}\setminus\left(U(\xi)\cup\mathbb{B}_{j}\right),\\[4.0pt]
M^{(r)}(z)M^{lo}(z)M^{(r)}(z)^{-1},&z\in\partial U(\xi),\\[4.0pt]
M^{(r)}(z)M^{B}_{j}(z)M^{(r)}(z)^{-1},&z\in\partial\mathbb{B}_{j},\end{array}\right.

(6.154)

see Figure
12
and Figure
13
. Especially we define

\Sigma^{(2)}=U(\xi):=\emptyset,\ \ \xi\in(-\infty,-1/8)\cup(1,+\infty).

\blacktriangleright
Singularities: As
z\rightarrow\varkappa_{l}=e^{\frac{i\pi(l-1)}{3}}
,
l=1,...,6
, the limit of
E(z;\xi)

has pole singularities with leading terms of a specific
matrix structure

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to\varkappa_{l}}E(z;\xi)=\lim_{z\to\varkappa_{l}}M^{R}(z)M^{B}_{l}(\varkappa_{l})M^{(r)}(z)^{-1}=\mathcal{O}((z-\varkappa_{l})^{-2}).

(6.155)

Here
E(z;\xi)
has both jump condition and singularities, its asymptotic behavior can be solved through
a small norm RH problem. A natural idea is to consider a matrix function

E^{(2)}(z;\xi)
which only has same jump with
E(z;\xi)
, but without pole singularities.

RHP 14
.

Find a matrix-valued function
E^{(2)}(z;\xi)
with following properties

\blacktriangleright
Analyticity:
E^{(2)}(z;\xi)
is analytical in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{E}(\xi)
, where

\displaystyle\Sigma^{E}(\xi)=\cup_{j=1}^{6}\partial\mathbb{B}_{j},\ \ \xi\in(-\infty,-1/8)\cup(1,+\infty),

\displaystyle\Sigma^{E}(\xi)=\left(\cup_{j=1}^{6}\partial\mathbb{B}_{j}\right)\cup\partial U(\xi)\cup(\Sigma^{(2)}\setminus U(\xi)),\ \ \xi\in(-1/8,1).

\blacktriangleright
Asymptotic behaviors:

\displaystyle E^{(2)}(z;\xi)\sim I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}),\hskip 14.22636pt|z|\rightarrow\infty;

(6.156)

\blacktriangleright
Jump condition:
E(z;\xi)
has continuous boundary values
E^{(2)}_{\pm}(z;\xi)
satisfying

E^{(2)}_{+}(z;\xi)=E^{(2)}_{-}(z;\xi)V^{E}(z),\ \ z\in\Sigma^{E}(\xi).

Re
z
e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}}
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}

\xi_{3}

The jump contour
\Sigma^{E}
for the

when

. The red circles are

, and orange square are

.

Re
z
e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}}

The jump contour

for the

when

. The orange square are
